Shelter History

Aeolus View Camp
LT mile 52.1 — Division 3 — Elev. 1950 G

A new shelter was built on the Bondville Road. east of Manchester. [The Green
Mountain News, Dec. 1922] The roof of the cabin on the Bondville road was
blown away and a new one put on. [The GM News, Dec. 1923] On the north side
of Motor Route 101 (present day route 30) which connects Manchester on the
west with BraTleboro on the southeast by way of Bondville is a Club shelter with
bunks for twenty to twenty-four persons. It commands a view of Mount Aeolus
near Manchester. [O'Kane 1926] It was very near the junc*on of Routes 11 and 30.
This is an open front camp with bunks for 16, stove and a *n roof. From this camp
one gets a charming view of Mt Aeolus framed in trees across the BaTenkill Valley.
[GB 9th Edi*on 1932] There is no men*on of this camp. only the view of Mt.
Aeolus framed in trees across the BaTenkill Valley. [GB 10th Edi*on 1935]


Aldrich Job Camp
Mile 1.5 — White Rock Sec*on — Elev. 1800 G

One remaining abandoned building west of trail and north of road aﬀords some
shelter. [GB 2nd Edi*on 1920] Not men*oned as a place for shelter in GB 8th
Edi*on 1930.


Aldrich Camp
This GMC camp is on the GB 5th Edi*on map but is not men*oned in the text. Not
men*oned as a place for shelter in GB 8th Edi*on 1930. I did not ﬁnd in GB text

<!-- page: 2 -->
that this camp was ever established, however. it was noted on GB 3rd Edi*on that
it was to be built in 1921.


Allen Camp
See Carmel Camp


Atlas Valley Shelter (3rd in this area)
Mile 11.8 — Division 12 — Elev. 2180 G

Located just north of the highway, this shelter. maintained by the GMC. is a small
lean-to of plywood and plywood cores. It has been provided for the use of hikers
by the Atlas Plywood Division over whose *mberlands this por*on of the trail
passes. It was fabricated at the company’s Morrisville plant and assembled on the
site by members of the GMC in 1967. Although not designed for overnight use. it
can accommodate 3 or 4 hikers, if necessary. Water is located across the highway
at a spring located on the LT. It has been in use from 1967 to present. [GB 23rd
Edi*on 1985]

Shortly before 10 am on Sept. 9, 1967, a truck from the Foss & Phelps Sawmill of
Morrisville came to a stop at the LT crossing on Route 242 south of Jay Peak. A
dozen or so people awaited the arrival and without delay they quickly transferred
the cargo to a carefully chosen spot located a few feet north of the road where
the Original Jay Camp was located. AGer the placing of several large rocks in a
square. a rugged plywood plajorm 8' square was taken from the truck and
expertly leveled. The pace quickened and two side walls. Then the rear wall was
ﬁTed precisely to the side walls. The one-piece plywood roof was a bit heavier
and more awkward but was also maneuvered into its appropriate loca*on. Lo and
behold! We were pulng a lean-to together! With all pieces in place, the new
structure was literally the center of a swarm of ac*vity. Up on the roof a crew was
busily laying down and nailing tar paper, while directly underneath other people
were ac*vely spiking and bol*ng everything down securely. Outside, a couple of
people were happily creoso*ng everything in sight, including the shelter: they
showed so much talent with a paint brush, in fact, that they were allowed to do
the inside as well. A drywall to keep out the porcupines was built. The shelter was

<!-- page: 3 -->
ﬁnished in early aGernoon. The shelter was designed and prefabricated in the
Morrisville Plant under the direc*on of the Company Forester, Warren B. Drown,
who also supervised the assembly. Mr. Drown emphasized the need for
recrea*onal use of its extensive northern Vermont *mberlands as an important
aspect of their mul*ple use management. [George Pearlstein. LT News, Nov. 1967]


Badger Camp
LT Mile 230.8 — Marjorie Hulburd Sec*on — Elev. 1800 G

This camp oﬀers shelter in the hayloG of the barn. There is no equipment at this
camp. [GB 6th Edi*on 1924] E. P. Dickinson noted in GB 7th Edi*on 1929 that this
was "dirty and forlorn, but roof good, lunched here Aug. '29." Camp is locked.
Shelter in hayloG of barn. No equipment. [GB 7th Edi*on 1929] This camp, old
house, and barn are in poor shape but aﬀord some shelter - not a GMC Camp. [LT
News, August 1931]


Barrows Camp
LT Mile 209.4 — Division 11 — Elev. 1440 G

This camp was built in 1931. It is a frame building with bunks for about 12. Water
at nearby spring. Good views of the Sterling Range. [GB 50th Anniversary Edi*on
1960 (16th)] It was in use from 1931 to around 1994 when Roundtop Shelter was
completed. Not men*oned in GB 24th Edi*on 1996. It was called Barrows Camp
as it was located on the Barrows Farm. [LT News, Feb. 1932]


Ba>ell Lodge (1st)
The President's Sec*on — Elev. 3300 G

Log cabin in bad repair aﬀords shelter, is apt to be dirty for picnickers. Stove inside
fair. No bunks. Simple cooking utensils. Fine water 1/8 mile south on trail. This
Lodge built by Col. Joseph BaTell in 1899 for use of his guests, ﬁTed complete
with everything needed by campers and kept open for their use. Looted and
wrecked by them. [GB 1st Edi*on 1917]

<!-- page: 4 -->
Ba>ell Lodge (2nd)
LT Mile 150.8 — Division 8 — Elev. 3400 G

A new open-front shelter built of peeled logs: good water, simple cooking utensils.
Old wagon road goes out to Lincoln Valley via farms of the Atkins Brothers. Built in
1926. [GB 7th Edi*on 1928] An open front shelter, built in 1928 of peeled logs by
the Forestry Dept of Middlebury College. It has stove, cooking utensils and bun/cs
for 10 or 12. There is a ﬁreplace against a big rock in front. Good water about 1/4
mile south near the Trail. [GB 9th Edi*on 1932] Announcement was made that the
shelter had recently burned, cause unknown. It is expected that this shelter will be
speedily rebuilt as it is much in demand for ﬁrst night lodging by those entering
the Trail from the Pass. [LT News. July 1937]


Ba>ell Shelter (3rd)
Mile 143.4 — Division 8 — Elev. 3300 G

This is a log structure built in 1938 by the LT Patrol for the New York Sec*on. It
provides bunk space for 6 to 8. Water is permanent at the spring 100 G to the
east. This shelter was once in a dense stand of large trees, but these were mostly
blown down by the hurricane of 1950. It was in use from 1938 to 1966. [GB 17th
Edi*on 1963] Cost of this rebuilt shelter was $194. [LT News. May 1939]

When we built BaTen Shelter (an open front Adirondack type built of logs), the
privy was built out of logs- the only log privy on the trail, I believe. We made a sign
iden*fying the shelter as BaTell Shelter and, when it came to naming the privy,
since it was enclosed instead of open front, the sign said, “BaTell Lodge.” (Camps
and lodges had four walls as opposed to shelters which had only three. Some
years later, a windstorm blew down several trees around BaTell, one of them
falling on the privy so it was leaning at a 35 or 40 degree angle. We played Lincoln
Logs, disassembled it, and reassembled it straight again.

For some reason that escapes me, perhaps for no reason, I gravitated to building
the privies at a number of camps. One that I recall was named Whiteface
Observatory. Another was named the “Defecatorium.” At one camp, we found a
tag that had apparently fallen out of a package of new underwear that somebody

<!-- page: 5 -->
had opened. It said "roomy balloon seat (sounds like a tall story) so we tacked it to
the privy seat. At another place. we were all electrical engineers — we painted an
ammeter on the seat with the needle on full discharge. [Reminiscences about Roy
Buchanan and the LT By Chet Buchanan, 1998]


Ba>ell Shelter (4th)
Mile 6.5 — Division 8 — Elev. 3250 G

This shelter was built in 1967 by campers from Farm and Wilderness Camp using
materials airliGed to the site. It sleeps 8. Water is from a small spring 100 G to the
east. A caretaker may be in seasonal residence to assist and educate hikers and
help protect the resources of the site and the summit of Mt. Abraham. A fee is
charged for overnight use. Wood ﬁres are not allowed within 500 G of this high-
eleva*on site. Ten*ng near BaTell Shelter is very limited. [GB 24th Edi*on 1996]
In July, work par*es from Tamarack Farm in Plymouth Union, Vt. began the
assembly of the shelter. This WITT, under the supervision of camp counselors, was
done for the Forest Service as a service project. The staﬀ of the GMNF thanks the
volunteers of Tamarack Farm for another new shelter. [LT News, November 1967]


Bear Hollow Shelter
Mile 22.4 — Division 10 — Elev. 1380 G

The shelter is perched on a rocky knoll above the trail. It was built by Bob
Lindemann and the Sterling Sec*on in 1991. This open-faced frame shelter sleeps
12. The water supply is a brook just south of the shelter along the LT. [GB 24th
Edi*on 1996] In use from 1991 to present.
Access for construc*on is via a wood road and snow mobile trail which passes
some very rus*c hun*ng camps. One of these camps is owned by Smith Edwards
who added a big eﬀort in the construc*on of this exclusive resort style shelter.

AGer the ini*al work to get the shelter framed up, Bob Lindemann and I did a lot
of the inside work. One weekend we built the bunks and slept in them so we
would be the ﬁrst to sleep in them. We also installed a very nice and substan*al
table. The next weekend a note in the logbook by the shelter adopter said, A very
nice table ... If I could ﬁnd a willing woman, I would ini*ate the table the right way.

<!-- page: 6 -->
The following weekend we found a message in the logbook saying, "He was too
late, she and "Big Boy" had ini*ated the table ... TWICE!". It was signed by
"Mountain Girl". The trail names have been changed to protect the guilty. [ORR.
May 15, 1999]

On Sept 28, 1991, with the ﬁrst snowfall on Mansﬁeld and Whiteface Mts., the
formal dedica*on of this shelter occurred. The dedica*on was preceded by a hike
from Smuggler's Notch. It replaces the French Camp which was about a mile north
of the new shelter. In aTendance were Fred Gilbert, Jeﬀ Harvey, George Bramhall,
Smith Edwards, Tony Smith, Elizabeth Bramhall, Cindy Bigelow, Bob Lindemann,
Paul and Joanne Woodward. [LTN Winter 1991]


Beaver Meadow Cabin
The President's Sec*on — Elev. 2650 G

South of Molly Stark, turn south from Balcony, gradually descend to big tree with
many arrows beside rooﬂess Beaver Meadow Cabin. [GB 1st Edi*on 1917] Half-
ruined log cabin gives some shelter. [GB 2nd Edi*on 1920] This cabin is not
men*oned in GB 6th Edi*on 1924.


Beaver Meadow Lodge
Mile 0.8 Chilcoot Trail — Division 10

The Sterling Sec*on built in 1947 a ﬁne log camp, called Beaver "Medder" Camp
about 1 1/4 miles directly south, and 900 G in eleva*on below Whiteface Shelter.
[GB 13th Edi*on 1947] A log cabin built by the Sterling Sec*on was completed in
1947. It has space for 15. Water is found in several adjacent brooks. In use from
1947 to present. [GB 24th Edi*on 1996]

From the "History of the Sterling Sec*on" - The new Sterling Sec*on organized on
January 27, 1946, in Morrisville with Henry Mould, one of Fred's sons, as its ﬁrst
president Spark for the group's forma*on was a plan to build a replacement lodge
for what by that *me was the somewhat bedraggled Mould Lodge. Work began in
May that year and throughout the balance of the warm weather months 12 to 15
Sec*on stalwarts (including Fred Mould on some of the trips) turned out almost

<!-- page: 7 -->
every Sunday to help erect a sturdy log cabin near the Beaver Meadow area from
which the new lodge took its name ... A horse was used to skid logs to the site and
some of the lumber came from nearby Mould Lodge which was dismantled.

It is very obvious, reading the Sterling Sec*on Ou*ng Log, 1971 to 1977, that the
GMC Sterling Sec*on people loved the outdoors, respected the beauty of the
Beaver Meadows area with its abundant wildlife and ﬂora, and made an enduring
eﬀort to care for the trails and shelters in this area of Vermont. The Beaver
Meadow Lodge, French Camp, and Whiteface Shelter had tender love and care
during these years under the direc*on of Robert Hagerman. A few excerpts from
the ou*ng log will make this very clear.

Snowshoes were leG at the door of the lodge ... a ﬁre was quickly started. As soon
as the lodge began to warm up, three or four spots in the roof near the chimney
began to leak, but soon slowed to almost nothing. However, for a few minutes,
Dick Spear was kept busy ... this is an indica*on that roof repair is needed. Before
depar*ng "the crew" collected dry dead wood from the "ghost forest"
surrounding the pond and piled it in the cabin ... The following is a list of work.
needed on the lodge:
    1. Fix roof leaks & inspect.
    2. Put in glass windows and in some cases new frames. Snow is blowing in
       window openings and cracks.
    3. Inspect and repair caulking where needed.
    4. New logbook.
    5. Check outhouse facili*es.
    6. Close out dump and put-up signs like the Killington sec*on has done,
       Garbage in, Garbage out "GiGo."
    7. Possible buy new keTle and pot for Lodge.
    8. General repairs around and to the building.
    9. Plant apple tree in the opening near the lodge for future spring*me beauty
       and harvest *me enjoyment.
[Brent Teillon 2/7/71]

Marking the downhill trek were several outbreaks of snowball ﬁghts. The Mar*ns
spoTed what appeared to be a large hawk perched in a tree at the very top edge
of the Notch cliﬀ. [Bob Hagerman 3/14/71]

<!-- page: 8 -->
On June 12, 1971, a large group gathered on a beau*ful Saturday at the
Morristown Corners store for a Beaver Meadow Lodge work trip ... At Beaver
Meadow Lodge:
  a) Repaired all windows, using Plexiglass on most of them.
  b) Screened over all windows (a porcupine was in the lodge when the group
      arrived)
  c) Put damper in stove pipe for new stove brought up previous year.
  d) Patched roof by tarring seams and several small holes.
  e) Caulked logs with oakum.
  f) Repaired one bench.
  g) Some rebuilding of outside ﬁreplace.
  h) Mounted "Beaver Meadow Lodge" sign on front wall which was provided by
      the Vt. Forest and Parks Dept.
  i) Cleaned up inside and leG new register.

Finally, what remained of the BM dump was carried out. The bulk of this was
carried out by a group of students at Sterling School, CraGsbury, and their leader
Ron Rosen on a special trip May 22. [Bob Hagerman 6/12/71]

Spring inspec*on Trip May 20, 1972: Selected site for outhouse to be built by a
group of Stowe High. School students as special project May 29 - June 2. AGer
much labor removing stones from hole in ground, planted one Delicious Apple
tree in open area in front of lodge. It immediately blossomed and bore fruit, 2
McIntosh apples and one orange! [Bob Hagerman 5/20/72]

Work Trip Oct.28, 1972: Another objec*ve not accomplished was the re-
iden*ﬁca*on of the boundaries of the ﬁve-acre plot of land around the BM Lodge
on which the Sterling Sec*on holds a lease. This was acquired in 1 S49 from Parker
and Steams Inc. of Johnson and calls for a payment of $5.00 per year as rent. A
map prepared by Willard Sanders gives the surveyed boundaries with the lodge
located at the center of the tract. No foreclosure is pending as the sec*on is paid
up on its rent! [Bob Hagerman 10/28/72]

Work Trip May 18, 1974: The following marks of vandals and thoughtless hikers
were found: good wooden sign iden*fying lodge had been stolen, a typewriTen
welcome no*ce was gone, two windows were broken (Plexiglass, not glass), and

<!-- page: 9 -->
several bundles of refuse were leG for someone else to carry out. Teillon and
Steven patched up the door of the outhouse. [Bob Hagerman 5/18/1974]

Work Trip June 14, 1975: Installed new stove pipe which had been carried in,
patched two holes in the ﬂoor; made other minor repairs, carried out three bags
of refuse ... apple tree looking quite healthy, though no apples, or even blossoms
yet. [Bob Hagerman 6/14/75]


       A Beaver Meadow Lodge History
       by Willard K. Sanders
       This log cabin, so well-equipped and so beau;fully situated, is a gi>
       from those names appearing below, to all who love the out-of-
       doors.
       Conceived in the winter of 1946. work commenced on its erec;on
       in May of the same year. The ground was cleared, logs were cut
       and peeled and skidded into place, cut, and shaped to true ﬁt, and
       lumber for the ﬂoor and roof, together with the rooﬁng paper,
       chinking material, stove, and furnishings were dragged or carried
       2.5 miles by hand from the nearest access road.
       During the building cows went un-milked, loved ones were
       forgoMen, ﬁsh poles, hitherto in constant use. stood in the closet
       gathering cobwebs, the family car spent many a Sunday parked in
       the ﬁeld at the end of the road, wedding anniversaries went by the
       board unno;ced, ﬁngernails were pounded, several new cuss-
       words were coined, false teeth were parked at home over Sunday,
       and interest in the Beaver Meadow Camp at ;mes overshadowed
       that of the Green River Dam, another small project currently in
       progress.
       Thus have the works of nature and man been blended into these
       pleasant surroundings; the ageless rocks of Whiteface look down
       upon this ediﬁce with approval, and Morse and Madonna Mts.
       shelter it in their bosoms from the tempests of the higher steeps;
       the ﬁrst morning rays of the sunshine with luster in the front door
       while the nearby brooks babble their gree;ngs.

<!-- page: 10 -->
May all who come to this spot appreciate the ;me and sacriﬁce
       which have made it possible, and help keep it in its present
       excellent condi;on, that it may con;nue to be a source of pride and
       pleasure to all who chance this way.
       Lucy Bagley, Harold Eaton, George “Doc” Goodrich, Morris Greene,
       Bobbie Jones, Clyde Limoge, Florence Mould, Fred Mould II, Richard
       Perkins, Neil Sargent, Jr., Charles Tinker, Paul Cornell, Judith
       Gilman, Frederic Greene, Glendora Hall, Walter D. Jones, Charles
       Mould, Fred Mould, Henry Mould, Willard K. Sanders, Ronald
       Terrill, Orrin Warren.



Belvidere Mountain Foresters' Cabin
LT Mile 237.3 Marjorie Hulburd Sec*on — Elev. 3360 G

Has good water and views. A side trail leads down the southern slope to an
interes*ng asbestos mine. [GB 7th Edi*on 1929] No men*on of lodging. [GB 10th
Edi*on 1935]


Big Branch Shelter
Mile 11.0 — Division 4 — Elev. 1470 G

The USFS, under the direc*on of Sidney B. Davis, has constructed this shelter at a
site 600 feet downstream from the new suspension bridge. It is on the proposed
trail reloca*on scheduled for comple*on in the spring of 1964. [LT News, Nov.
1963]

Con*nuing alongside Big Branch, the Trail follows an old road, passes the stone
founda*on of a mill, and reaches Big Branch Shelter. This frame lean-to, with ﬂoor
space for was built by the USFS in 1963. Big Branch furnishes water. The outhouse
is-up the hill behind the shelter. [GB 24th Edi*on 1996] It has been in use from
1963 to present.

<!-- page: 11 -->
Bigelow Shelter
Mile 9.6 on Lye Brook Trail StraTon Pond — Division 3 — Elev.2555 G

A log lean-to with ﬂoor space for 6. It was built by the Worcester Sec*on and
named in honor of Louis L Bigelow, for many years an ac*ve trail worker in the
Worcester Sec*on. Water is from Bigelow Spring, 150 G east of the shelter on the
trail. [GB 24th Edi*on 1996] This shelter is located on the Lye Brook Trail on the
south shore of StraTon Pond just 0.1 mile west of the LT. This shelter is in one of
the heaviest usage areas of the LT system and is managed by a caretaker located
at the Willis Ross clearing. Used from 1961 to 1997. It was dismantled in 1997. It
was built to replace the Willis Ross Annex which was ﬂooded due to beaver
ac*vity at the outlet which raised the water level about 1 foot. It was started in
August 1961, and ﬁnished the ﬁrst part of October 1961. [LT News, Nov. 1961]


Birch Glen Lodge (1st)
LT Mile 162.3 — Division D — Elev. 2020 G

In 1913 Miss Emily Proctor provided a fund through which three log lean-tos were
erected. The shelter construc*on of the exis*ng LT was started at this *me.

The approach trail to Birch Glen begins at the farm buildings and crosses a pasture
where the way is marked by cairns. Entering woods and passing through a fence
the trail becomes a well-marked woodland path. At the end of a mile from the
Beane Place it arrives at this lodge on the LT, one of the shelters maintained by the
GMC. The lodge is in an open forest of beech and yellow birch with occasional
towering spruces. To the right as you approach it, there is a brook which can be
depended upon to furnish a supply of water, even in *mes of drought. The shelter
has four double bunks and is provided with a small wood stove and a convenient
table. The loca*on is well described by the name given to the lodge, Birch Glen.
[O'Kane 1926]

(7 7-10 mile, 1900 G) where there is a birch lodge enclosed on three sides, facing
northwest, which will sleep 5 or 6 people: no caretaker, ﬁreplace a few feet in
front: some simple cooking utensils on hanging shelf in lodge. Permanent stream,
good water 50 G south of lodge; one place to dip water for oinking& cooking;
place for bathing further downstream. [GB 1st Edi*on 1917] They were very

<!-- page: 12 -->
concerned about water pollu*on in 1917 as everyone is today. Giardia lamblia
probably was not in the dic*onary at this *me, however. By 1922 this lodge is an
open camp with double-deck bunks for 12, good stove and simple cooking
utensils. [GB 4th Edi*on 1922]


Birch Glen Camp (2nd)
LT Mile 169.4 — Division 9 — Elev. 2020 G

This log structure, built in 1930 by GMC volunteers, has an open front "living
room" and semi-enclosed sleeping quarters with bunk space for 12. The water
source is a brook 100 G south. The shelter is located only 100 G down the Beane
Trail which leads 1.5 miles west to Carse Road. Used from 1930 to present. [GB
24th Edi*on 1996] It is expected that the new cabin at Birch Glen will be ready
about the Fourth of July. It will be a log structure of ample propor*ons. A slab
lining will tend to make the cabin considerably warmer and more weatherproof
than the average shelter. It will also embody several unique features that will
provide more adequate ven*la*on and ligh*ng of the bunks. The cabin, which will
aﬀord comfortable sleeping accommoda*ons for from sixteen to twenty, will be
equipped with stove and cooking utensils. It will be close to an adequate water
supply .and within easy walking distance of Hanksville, with its shopping and mail
facili*es. [LT News, Apr. 1929]

AGer many years of chronic roof leak, the roof ridge pole was raised 4.5 feet which
increased the slope to 4.5 feet in 11 feet of roof. The roof was also reversed to
avoid the eaves to ridge pole drainage. [LT News, Nov. 1961] The bunks were
lengthened so now it sleeps 12 instead of 4 to 6. The sleepers can be the same
length as Don Wallace as they are now 6'6" long. Don's foot overhang is now less
than a foot [LT News, August 1965]

The oldest shelter on the LT will be renovated in September 1999. In July. Director
of Field Programs Dave Hardy, Field Supervisors Greg Western and Seth Coﬀey, LT
Patrol Crew Leader John BenneT, restora*on contractor Jan Lewandoske, and
restora*on architect Mary Jo Llewelyn, visited the site and discussed repair
op*ons. What was likely the original metal rooﬁng was tacked to the sides of the
log walls, and with not a liTle trepida*on. we removed this crude but eﬀec*ve
porky-prooﬁng. The logs underneath are in great shape! The sill logs, however,

<!-- page: 13 -->
suﬀered from forty to seventy years of neglect and were completely roTen, as was
the ﬂoor. In September the LT Patrol will replace the log sills, ﬂooring, and bunks.
The structure's age will make it a delicate opera*on. This year's work combined
with the rooﬁng done a few years ago by Tim Tierney should give Birch Glen
another 70 years on the LT. [LT News, Fall 1999]


Blowdown Camp
This is a lumbering camp about 0.5 mile from Bread Loaf Inn in Middlebury Gap.


Blue Triangle Camp
LT Mile 90.5 — Division 5 — Elev. 1850 G

This cabin was recently erected. It is a closed frame construc*on suitable for 12
and is the giG of the Blue Triangle Club of the State YMCA. Located in the woods
on the bank of the crook. [GB 9th Edi*on 1932.] No men*on of this camp in GB
13th Edi*on 1947. This roomy cabin (20 x24 G) has been completed. It is located
near the Gov. Clement Camp and cost $550. Girls’ Clubs of the Longfellow School
of Rutland, of Wallingford, Wells River, Newbury, Thejord, Chester, Montpelier,
Bristol, Franklin, Enosburg Falls, Springﬁeld, St Albans, and Windsor Scouts
contributed toward its cost. It is intended more for use of the girls when on the
Trail. [LT News, October 1930] The only cabin reported seriously damaged by the
Sept 21, 1938, storm. [LT News. Nov. 1938] The abandoned Shelter will be
disassembled and moved to Spring Lake this summer. [LT News, Feb. 1949]


Bolton Lodge
LT Mile 181.6 — Division 9 — Elev. 1540 G

This lodge, situated in a clearing, provides a ﬁne view of Camel's Hump. This ﬁne
and comfortable cabin, built of stone and stucco to discourage porcupines, was
erected in 1928 by the Burlington Sec*on of the GMC. It has a good stove and
bunks for 12 or more hikers. [G8 8th Edi*on 1930] It was abandoned aGer trail
reloca*on because of Bolton Valley Ski Area. It was not men*oned GB 22nd
Edi*on 1983.

<!-- page: 14 -->
The new lodge on Bolton Mountain is probably the best constructed and most
invi*ng camp yet built on the Trail It takes the place of the old Dunsmoor Lodge. It
is designed aGer the coTages of Wales and Ireland, four feet of cobble stone at
the base, white stucco above, and a four-sided roof with round corners. Red and
black shingles trimmed irregularly give the eﬀect of a thatched root Green on the
door and window frames adds a bright touch. The interior measures 15 by 25 feet,
with bunks for twelve. The ﬂoor consists of ﬂat stones set in cement, and the
furniture is of white birch. It is warranted porcupine proof. The site was selected
by Mr. E. S. Bryant, owner of the mountain. The view from the front is of
surpassing beauty, with the Couching Lion in the center of the picture. A few yards
away is a spring and a liTle farther, the tumultuous Joiner Brook. Special credit is
due to Dr. E. G. Twitchell and Ernest Smith and to the volunteer workers who
contributed many days of skilled labor. The cost of the building was $1,100. from
numerous sources. The name has not yet been decided. [LT News, Dec. 1928]

New Year's Eve at Bolton. Among the ten sec*ons of the GMC there exists no ﬁner
tradi*on than this annual party of the Burlington Sec*on. While the rest of the
country are too*ng horns and swinging in a packed dance hall, the Burlingtonians
quietly roll up blankets and set out for Bolton Lodge late on the last day of the
year. Ordinarily the best skiing for the Burlington Sec*on centers about this Lodge.
It has miles of trails and a cabin that can be comfortably heated even in severe
weather. Lack of snow provided a departure from the usual rou*ne this year. Ice-
creeping was in order this year. By nine-thirty 16 had arrived aGer doing 3.5 miles
of pack-juggling up the trail.

Normally a group of hikers who have tramped that distance are ready to have taps
sounded at ten, but the spirit triumphs even on the trail. The last hours of 1940
were spent hilariously in sal*ng down tall tales of hikes, an in*mate reading by
Professor Buchanan of one hiker's experiences on the trail, and in stowing away a
sack of shell peanuts that someone had brought. Every Yankee has to keep his
hands or jaws busy on such an occasion, and in the absence of enough whiTling to
go around, the peanuts did the trick. Then as the minute hand of Professor
Buchanan's watch crept up on midnight, the 1940 calendar was ceremoniously
brought out and its tradi*onal burning took place as Indian yells and whoops
reverberated through the raGers.

<!-- page: 15 -->
The bedding down a half hour later brought out some serious accommoda*on
problems. The lodge was built to take care of a dozen. There were sixteen of us.
With a liTle jackkniﬁng, the bunks were made to suﬃce for fourteen, and two
others compromised by stretching out on the ﬂoor and benches. Ten more club
followers driGed in the next day bringing the roster to 26 and making as ﬁne a
lounging and hike company as any lodge ever entertained. Silng on one of the
lodge bunks, with my feet dangling over the edge, New Year's Day, I turned over
the thought that this group surely exempliﬁes true out-right democracy, all ages
and professions mingling together in complete enjoyment, each mindful and
anxious to help the other enjoy the day - such an atmosphere made the Long Trail
possible. In the aGernoon as the group picked their icy way home, the clouds
liGed lelng the slant rays of the sun ﬁlter through. Along the area of frost-
encrusted, ﬁr-covered slopes, the sun cast a ribbon-like streak of gold, and where
the slopes were untouched by the ribbon, a sheen of pink and purple was
reﬂected - the perfect benedic*on to a grand party and for a new year. [Larry
Dean, Jan. 1940. LT News, Feb. 1941]

On the weekend of June 7, 1958, the Burlington Sec*on did an extensive repair
job. They had 24 people helping on both days. They rebuilt about 3 feet of brick
chimney, replaced several roof boards, shingled the whole front half of the roof,
replaced chimney ﬂashing, re-shingled near the chimney, put in two completely
new windows, put in new boards for a whole bunk and 1/2 of another, replaced
two seTee backs, built up a toilet founda*on, replaced the inside of a toilet, and
cleaned up the area. Quite a job! [Roy Buchanan. LT News, August 1958]
Vandalism has occurred. About half of the windowpanes have been broken this
spring. [LT News, Nov. 1963]


Bourne Pond Camp (1st)
LT Mile 48.9 — Division 3 — Elev. 2550 G

The Pond is one of great beauty, full of trout; and with three camps at the inlet or
south end, one of which is open for all comers, and provided with beds and

It is not a GMC camp. There are boats on Bourne Pond but none on StraTon. [GB
4th Edi*on 1922] not for hiker use in GB 9th Edi*on 1932. Men*oned as a private
camp and apparently.

Boyce Lodge (1st)

Men*oned in O'Kane, 1926, it was located probably less than 112 miles from the

Boyce Shelter (2nd)
Mile 3.5 — Division 7 — Elev. 2930 G

This log structure was built in 1941 by the LT Patrol for the Lake Pleiad Sec*on. It

Boyce Shelter (3rd)
Mile 13.0 — Division 7 — Elev. 3020 G

This frame shelter, with space for 8, was built by the US Forest Service in 1963.

I used this shelter only days aGer it was open for public use. We had bypassed the
shelter at Lake Pleiad aGer a long day because of no water and a high

Bread Loaf Glen Shelter (1st)
The 2nd & 3rd was Emily Proctor Lodge in Breadloaf Glen

In 1913 Miss Emily Proctor provided funds for a log lean-to shelter to be built here

Bread Loaf Shelter (1st)
LT Mile 131.4 — Division 7 — Elev. 3300 G

A log structure built by the Lake Pleiad Sec*on in 1931 has bunks for about 10.

Bromley Lodge (1st)
LT Mile 52.9 — Division 3

This lodge, built by the Manchester Sec*on in 1933; very comfortable lodge on a

popular with trail travelers, one man liking it so well that he made it his summer

Bromley Camp (2nd)
Mile 18.2 — Division 3 — Elev. 2080 G

This frame structure was built by the LT Patrol in 1955. There were bunks for 8. It

Bromley TenLng Area (3rd)
Mile 18.2 — Division 3 — Elev. 2080 G

Two tent plajorms are located here. A communal ﬁreplace and outhouse are
located here. It is the former site of Bromley Camp which was removed in 1984. In

<!-- page: 18 -->
use from 1985 to present. [GB 24th Edi*on_ 1996] The GMC is inves*ga*ng a new
site for this ten*ng area. [LTN Fall 1997]


Bryant Camp
In the upper clearing before reaching Bolton Lodge, a ﬁne cabin belonging to Mr.
E. S. Bryant of Bolton, is used as a ski camp in winter; it is not open to hikers
unless special arrangement is made with Mr. Bryant.

The Burlington Sec*on has not been wholly outdone for camps; it has had its
Camp Bryant. One of the sec*on's members, Edward S. Bryant, opened his ﬁne ski
camp near the LT in Bolton to the exclusive use of the Burlington Sec*on these
past two seasons. The camp has become increasingly popular for both skiers and
snowshoes as well as plain relaxers of the sec*on. The sec*on also staged its ﬁrst
bird study hike of the season at the camp early this spring. [LT News, May 1945]


Buchanan Lodge
Mile 4.4 Bamforth Ridge Trail — Division 9 — Elev. 800 G

See Wiley Lodge – Same Lodge

This frame cabin with bunks for about 12, was built by the LT Patrol in 1949.
Adjacent Gleason Brook furnishes water. Formerly Wiley Lodge, the cabin was
renamed Buchanan lodge in 1964 upon the comple*on of the new Wiley Lodge on

men*oned in the 22nd Edi*on of GB 1983.


Buchanan Shelter
Mile 26.6 — Division 9 — Elev. 2310 G

This shelter was named for Prof. Roy O. Buchanan, founder of the Long Trail Patrol

open front porch and enclosed bunk room with space for 16. Water is located 100

Wm started on the ﬁrst weekend in May. Don Wallace and Jack Lance of the

Buﬀum Camp (1st)
LT Mile 79.3 — Division 5 — Elev. 1600 G

Crossing the highway (Rte. 140) the LT follows an old public road uphill for about
3/4 mile. Then proceeds a few minutes' walk to this Club shelter with bunks for 20

water 30 G west. [GB 50th Anniversary Edi*on 1960 (16th)] Through the
generosity of Mr. Merton C. Fisher of New Bedford. Mass., new rooﬁng has been
applied to this shelter. Early last summer Mr. Fisher visited the shelter and no*ced
that the paper rooﬁng was badly torn. He wrote asking permission to have it ﬁxed.
Now Buﬀum shelter is in good condi*on again. We need more people like Mr.
Fisher. [LT News, May 1950]


Buﬀum Lodge (2nd)
LT Mile 74.4 — Division 5 — Elev. 1600 G

A frame cabin with bunks for 12 to 16 was built by the Killington Sec*on in 1961.
Brook water 30 G west. [GB 17th Edi*on 1963] This ﬁne cabin was destroyed by

Butler Lodge
Mile 7.3 — Division 10 — Elev. 3040

The lodge is of log construc*on. It was erected by the LT Patrol in 1933. It is
named for Mabel Taylor Butler who was member of the Burlington Sec*on and a
lover of the Green Mountains. There are bunks and loG space for 14. Water is
available at a small brook 75 G to the east and at *mes can be unreliable. During
the hiking season a GMC caretaker is in residence to educate and assist hikers and
maintain the site and nearby trails. A fee is charged for overnight use. Wood ﬁres
are prohibited. It has been in use from 1933 to present. [GB 24th Edi*on 1996]
Now has a new stove, thanks to the eﬀorts of eight Burlington sec*oneers, Harris
AbboT, Andy Thomas, Carlene Whitcomb, Ray Bard, John Sharp, Jack: Lance, and
Rod and Rick Rice. In mid-February the group hauled in a 45-pound stove made by
another GMC'er, Lyman Howard, plus ﬁve sec*ons of stovepipe. Traveling on
snowshoes, they used a small plas*c sled to transport the stove to Butler where it
was installed and, we trust, properly inaugurated with a good ﬁre. [LT News, May
1977]

The Burlington Sec*on, led by Dana Baron and Leo Leach, have begun planning
next year's renova*on of Butler lodge. Gary Sawyer has cu1 logs donated by the
State. and the Burlington Sec*on Work: par*es have peeled forty logs for the
project. No one has donated the helicopter liG, so a fair chunk of our donated
funds will be used to ﬂy in logs. Fred Gilbert is working on an interior design and
plans are underway to hire a small crew to work with Sec*on volunteers next
summer. Plans include rebuilding the founda*on. A griphoist project will be
conducted to accumulate rock: for this part of the job. Windows may be replaced,
but window and door size will remain the same. Butler was roofed four years ago
so rerooﬁng is not a concern. [LTN Fall 1999]


Camel's Hump Clearing Huts
Mile 10.2 — Division 9 — Elev. 3800 G

<!-- page: 21 -->
This was the site, over a century ago, of a rus*c frame hotel (summit house), built
about 1860, which failed ﬁnancially and ﬁnally burned down in 1875. In 1908 the
Camel's Hump Club was organized. The old path was opened and improved, tents
for lodging were provided. The clearing was later adopted for three *n huts that
provided shelter for hikers from 1912 un*l the early 1950's. A caretaker was on
duty and trampers were made welcome. There are three metal buildings, one to
the leG occupied by the caretaker, one straight ahead which serves as quarters for
women trampers, and one to the right which contains the bunks for men. The
records show that 700 persons climbed the Hump in 1910 and many of them
stayed overnight. [O'Kane 1926]

On June 20, 1908, a group of local businessmen came together to form the
Camel's Hump Club, with the purpose of developing Camel's Hump as a mountain
resort that would appeal to mountain walkers. C. C. Graves was elected President.
The ﬁrst, and possibly the only major accomplishment of the Camel's Hump Club,
was the provision of camping facili*es just below the summit of the Hump. At
ﬁrst, four large tents were furnished at the clearing, three of them big enough for
12 people each and one monstrosity that slept 20. Each tent was equipped with
stove, lantern, dishes and utensils, a supply of foodstuﬀs, "fragrant boughs for
bedding," and "clean, new blankets. In 191 O this ten*ng area aTracted 700
visitors, the following year 1,100. In 1912 a 14' by 20' hut was erected at the site.
It, in no sense, oﬀers the ease and luxuries of the great houses of other mountain
resorts, conceded the Camel's Hump Club. [Forest & Crag by Laura & Guy
Waterman, 1989]

Lodging 60 cents per night which includes one blanket, extra ones 10 cents each.
[GB 2nd Edi*on 1920] Two metal huts are men*oned and no caretaker. [GB 14th
Edi*on 1951] three huts remain. [GB 15th Edi*on 1956]


Camp #4
The ﬁrst trip across Bolton Mountain to locate a trail was also made in October
1910 by Mr. Cowles and Mr. Burt. Star*ng from the Nebraska Notch road near the
Trout Club, the route lies along an old logging rod nearly to the summit of Mt
Admiral Clarke; one of the outlaying peaks of the mass of Bolton Mountain and

<!-- page: 22 -->
arrives at the summit; 4 miles. Some judicious lumbering on the heavily wooded
summit would improve the situa*on by opening up some ﬁne views of Mt.
Mansﬁeld and Camel's Hump. The trail con*nues south from the summit soon
ﬁnding Bolton Brook which if follows to the Winooski River. Two miles down the
trail an abandoned lumber camp known as Camp No. 4 oﬀers shelter, and two
miles below this are found the mill and buildings of a lumber company now
closed. [The Making of the Long Trail by Lewis J. Paris, M.D.]

This is the ﬁrst reference to a GMC shelter that I can ﬁnd. It may be the same
place or camp as Dunsmoor Lodge, however, that is doubjul. O'Kane says that
Camp #4 was 1.2 miles south of Bolton summit. Dunsmoor Lodge is listed in
numerous places as 2 miles north of Bolton Lodge and the hiking distance
between Bolton Lodge and the Summit in that era was 3.6 miles, which tells me
that there were two diﬀerent places .4 miles apart. Laura and Guy Waterman say
that Camp #4 and Dunsmoor Lodge are the same. Whether they were the same
place or not, Camp #4 on the south slope of Bolton Mountain was the ﬁrst GMC
shelter which wasn't a care-taken summit camp that catered to the public. [Paul
Woodward 4/17/99]


Camp Cobb (1st Worcester SecLon Clubhouse)
For many years members of the Worcester Sec*on have dreamed of having a
camp of our own. Now, when "Where to go" has become a major problem for
most ou*ng clubs, Dr. Gardner N. Cobb, a charter member of this sec*on, has
given our club ﬁve years free use of a building on his large estate. Camp Cobb, as it
is now called, was originally built as a hun*ng hut for a group of businessmen.
Long since neglected, it was full of cobwebs, dust, squirrel nests, and old
furniture. The ﬂoor, walls, and roof were in good condi*on, and aGer a couple of
work par*es the camp had a new paper lining, some shiny white doors, clean
window, and kitchen range — the giG of a father of two members, both of whom
are now in the service.

The Grand Opening" came last November. Thirty-seven members came out for a
long hike over the nearby trails and ended up with a baked bean supper. Since
then, electric lights have been put in; and the old well has been uncovered,
cleaned, had a new frame built for it, and has been equipped with an old-

<!-- page: 23 -->
fashioned well sweep and copper-bound oaken bucket. Thanks for most of the
work goes to Duane O. Sprague, House CommiTee Chairman, and to George F. E.
Story, Honorary Life Member of the Worcester Sec*on. Duane and George delight
in gelng surprises ready for the next scheduled ou*ng.

The picnic nook has been selected. An outdoor ﬁreplace will be built, under Louis
L Bigelow's supervision, as soon as the weatherman permits. From the center of
the city, a twenty-minute bus ride and a mile walk require liTle eﬀort for the
rewards received. Many miles of trails are accessible from Camp Cobb — steep
trails or easy ones, trails through dense woods or over open hill tops, trails along
the side of a babbling brook or on the edge of a high cliﬀ, and a view that
demands aTen*on from every vantage point.
Keys to the camp have been given to several members who are easily contacted.
Any other member may get a key and have a private trail jaunt at his convenience.
A short *me ago our main object was to keep our GMC sec*on together for the
Boys to come home to. But with less gas for long trips and such a grand place
nearby, our average aTendance is growing. Now we look forward to giving a
hearty welcome to new members, and a Bang-up Camp Cobb recep*on to each of
our Boys and Girls on their return. ["The Realiza*on of a Dream" by Jessie
Phillimore; LT News, April 1944]

Three ou*ngs were scheduled at Camp Cobb. One, a trail tour, was unique,
because, even with the man-power shortage, more men than women aTended.
Another, at which a pot-luck supper was planned, found a few hardy folks who
traveled a very bad storm to have a good *me and a ﬁne feast. At the third, 27
enjoyed all types of winter sports and then ended the aGernoon by devouring a
beef stew supper, the supplies for which were lugged in on toboggan over the last
half mile. A Christmas Party and the Annual Dinner Mee*ng ﬁlled out the
program. At the Annual Mee*ng aTended by 73 members and guests, it was
unanimously voted to make Louis L Bigelow an Honorary Life Member of the
Worcester Sec*on. This honor was conferred on Louis in recogni*on of the many
years that he has worked (or made the other fellows work!) on the trails for which
the Sec*on is responsible. [LT News, February 1945]

Another big event for us. Arriving on Saturday aGernoon for a weekend at camp.
Before sundown a forty-foot ﬂagpole was set up, more parking space was cleared
Oust in case!!) and our wood pile had grown considerably. Plans for a LT wat party

<!-- page: 24 -->
were formulated. On Sunday a grand dinner prepared by three members was
enjoyed between hikes, and "Old Glory" was oﬃcially raised over Camp Cobb. [LT
News, August 1945] We regrejully leave this camp not later than May 1, 1948. [LT
News; Feb. 1948]


Camp Eleven
Southeast of Glastenbury Mt summit en route to Somerset Reservoir. Small cabin
has bunks for 3 or 4 person, stove and few cooking utensils. Camp not locked. [GB
1st Ed. 1917] This is one small shack leG here from an old lumber camp, but at the
present *me not habitable except for hedgehogs. [GB 3rd Edi*on 1921]


Camp Lizzie
Two miles from the Buck Job there is a side trail leading downhill to the east about
1/4 mile to a ruined lumber camp, one cabin of which is in excellent repair and
open; at the conﬂuence of two amber-colored streams of ﬁne drinking water, it is
marked Camp Lizzie and is the headquarters of gatherers of medicinal herbs,
roots, and barks. Has beds and bedding, stove and utensils but is not a GMC camp.
[GB 4th Edi*on 1922]


Camp 3/D
Later called Camp Clarke. 2nd Worcester Sec*on Clubhouse.

The Worcester Sec*on now has three camps, Willis Ross, Camp Cobb, and now
this one. It is located in the rear of the home of Herbert M. Dockham, Holden,
Mass. Dock has always wanted a back-yard camp, and now he has it. Nestled in
the woodland beyond his garden he has built an open front shelter large enough
for forty people to sit down to a meal at the portable tables. He has aﬃxed a
green pine-tree shaped sign with white leTers which reads GMC Worcester
Sec*on Camp 3/D. Knowing how hard it is for a hungry person to wait his tum to
gill a steak, Dock has built a ﬁreplace about 12 feet long with three separate
sec*ons. so that a dozen people may cook at the same *me. As he had just about

<!-- page: 25 -->
given up his search for enough grates, a group of GMC'ers cropped in for a cook-
out supper and brought along gates for two openings as a birthday giG. His
special, joy is the "Deacon Seat" lined up on one side of the approach facing the
ﬁreplace. Unlike the LT shelters, Camp 31D sports electric lights and has a large
overhead light outside. [LT News, Aug. 1946] August 10, 1956, camp destroyed by
ﬁre. Damages es*mated at $4,000. The building contained a kitchen and large
mee*ng room. [LT News. Nov. 1956]


Carmel Camp (1st)
Mile 0.2 South Killington Sec*on. — Elev. 2800 G

Some*mes known as Allen Camp. [GB 2nd Edi*on 1920]


Carmel Camp (2nd)
LT Mile 115.5 Killington Sec*on — Elev. 2800 G

This is an oblong, closed building, with walls and roof of metal. Across the front is
a piazza. Within there are bunks to sleep 6 people, and other furnishings,
including a good stove, table, bench, and some utensils. There are no blankets.
The camp stands in a gassy opening partly gown up to raspberry bushes. A branch
path to the leG as you enter the opening leads to a good spring. The distance from
New Boston to the camp is about a mile and a half and the rise in al*tude is about
650 feet. [O'Kane 1926]

Built in 1921 on the ruins of the former camp. [GB 4th Edi*on 1922] This camp is
now (spring 1947) in a collapsed unusable condi*on but will be rebuilt by the club
as soon as materials and labor can be obtained. [GB 13th Edi*on 1947] Post War
on the Long Trail. The blazes will need poin*ng on almost the whole LT. Many
signs must be replaced where the old ones have been removed. Three camps;
Carmel, Cooley Glen, and Hazen's Notch are down; a number of others are so old
and dilapidated that they will require extensive repairs or replacement soon. [LT
News, May 1945]


Carmel Camp (3rd)

<!-- page: 26 -->
Mile 13.1 — Division 6 — Elev. 2620 G

David Logan Shelter is 4th shelter in this loca*on.

This camp was built in 1949. It was a small steel building with bunks for a

permiTed except during the deer hun*ng season when it is reserved for its
owners' use and may be locked. It was torn down in 1976 and replaced by the

When I stayed overnight in 1963, it had been raining most of the day. In order to
dry out a bit, we built a ﬁre and *died up the place. We knew we were in trouble
when we got there as we saw a couple of quilly characters. That night they were
on the roof doing their thing un*l we threw a 2-inch ﬁrecracker up to their party,
aGer which they went back to the dump. [Paul Woodward]


Cascade Shelter
See Duck Brook Shelter

A small log shelter built by the boys of Camp Najerog in 1931. It has bunks for 5

Major repairs were made to the Shelter, logs in rear replaced, roof nailed, and all

May, 19TT]


Cedar Meadow Camp

A Worcester Sec*on camp on the shores of Cedar Meadow Lake in Leicester has

ChaTield's Boarding House
Located on approach trail from South Lincoln via Lewis Chajield's, hence autos

Clarendon Camp (1st)
LT Mile 84.8 — Division 5 — Elev. 1350 G

This camp is situated in a hillside pasture near a brook with accommoda*ons for
20 persons. [O’Kane 1926] This camp has a stove and bunks for twenty people.
This is on a brook and in a pasture; keep porch gate closed on account of caTle.
[GB 7th Edi*on 1928] This shelter is an old favorite of many in the Killington
Sec*on. It was built in 1921 and has weathered many a storm. Now it is old and
leaky, but s*ll a welcome sight to many who hike over the LT. It is situated in a

card party is planned to raise money for major repairs at the home of Vera Perkins
in Rutland. [LT News, Feb. 1952]


Clarendon Shelter (2nd)
Mile 6.3 — Division 5 — Elev. 1350 G

This framed building with bunks for 12 was constructed in 1952 by the Killington
Sec*on. A brook 50 G east furnishes water. In use from 1952 to present. [GB 24th
Edi*on 1996] The new closed shelter completed during this past summer has
been formally rededicated as Clarendon Lodge. Come no more querulously
seeking Clarendon Lean-to. Not only does it not now lean to leeward of every
passing wind ...it is solid, substan*al, and winter-proof. It took 374 hours of labor.

<!-- page: 28 -->
Hikers were using it prior to comple*on as were the cows. [LT News, Nov. 1952]
Mr. Wiley donated $250 toward the rebuilding of this shelter. [LT News, Feb. 1953]
Due to misuse, this was converted to an open-faced shelter in 1971. On May 15,
1971, 40 people were present to do the carpentering. On the way out they hauled
a full pick-up full of trash which had accumulated since last Sept. They had to
clean up two 50-year-old pines that had been destroyed by misusers of the
shelter. A new privy was built with recycled wood taken from the front of the
lodge. The fence around the shelter is to keep out the cows in summer. [LTN
August 1971]


Cloudland Shelter
Mile 7.5 — Division AT2 — Elev. 1480 G

It has space for 8 and was built by the Dartmouth College Ou*ng Club in 1995.
Ample water is available in a nearby stream. It has been in use from 1995 to
present. [GB 24th Edi*on 1996]


Congdon Shelter/Camp
Mile 10 — Division 1 — Elev. 2080 G

This open-front frame cabin, with bunks for 8, was built by the LT Patrol in 1967. It
was named for Herbert Wheaton Congdon, a Long Trail pioneer, trail builder and
mapmaker, and was the giG of the Congdon family. A small brook east of the

the LT above the outhouse. It was originally built as a cabin and converted to an
open shelter aGer 1985. In 1985 there was a Primi*ve Camping Area 0.8 mile
north of the shelter which had been abandoned by 1996. [GB 24th Edi*on 1996]
This camp was built between July 31 and August 11, 1967. Its construc*on was
headed up by Roy Buchanan & Jack Harrington.


       In the summer of 1981, I was caretaker at Congdon Camp,
       southernmost caretaker site on the Long Trail. I maintained 14
       miles of trail, south to Seth Warner Shelter and north to Melvin
       Nauheim Shelter. I was 17.

<!-- page: 29 -->
I was paid a lump sum of maybe $500. for the whole summer. I also
       got to pocket whatever fees I collected from people who stayed at
       the shelter, which I believe was $1.50 per person per night. But not
       many people stayed there, so I didn't make too much, and ended
       up ea;ng a lot of generic mac and cheese. I also collected
       returnable cans for money. Caretakers at more popular sites didn't
       get a lump sum. They did ﬁne just pocke;ng overnight fees. Clearly
       these folks could aﬀord a more diverse diet.
       It was lonely at ;mes. Congdon was no StraMon or Camel's Hump.
       O>en it was just me, the porcupines, and the woodpecker that liked
       to knock on the side of the cabin at 5 AM. I read books, swaMed
       insects, picked berries, and explored the woods. Harmon Hill, with
       its view to the west, was my favorite spot.
       Some people did come through, I met plenty of AT through hikers,
       who were, of course, not used to paying an overnight fee. One
       night I had a whole camp group of fairly well-behaved kids huddled
       in the cabin to escape the rain. I slept under the table and made a
       personal record $20.
       Some;mes I'd meet up with the caretaker from Goddard Shelter at
       route 9 and play chess. My major excitement was a weekly trek
       into cosmopolitan Bennington on my bicycle. I'd load up on Kurt
       Vonnegut books at the library and cheap carbohydrates at the A&P.
       Back then, Congdon was a four walled cabin with a wood stove. It
       has since been converted to a lean-to. I believe they were having
       trouble with some guy living there through the winter.”
       I learned a lot and grew as a person at Congdon Camp that
       summer. it was a challenging rite of passage that I would
       recommend to anyone.
       [Robert A. Woodward, 7/10/98]


This shelter, which is really a nice, closed camp, was a giG to the GMC from the
Congdon Family in memory of Herbert Wheaton Congdon (1876-1965). Mr.
Congdon was an ac*ve member and trustee for many years. He made many of the

<!-- page: 30 -->
early maps of the LT and was contribu*ng author to the book "Footpath in the
Wilderness." He wrote interes*ngly of the wildﬁre on the trail. Praise be, one of
the delights of our trail is the absence of danger from anything but our own
carelessness. His keen feeling for the trail was unmistakable. Leisureliness. taking
*me to see and hear thoughjully and intelligently, studying to be quiet, opens
new vistas that lead to serenity. All at once the quiet of the wilderness comes
alive, the silence is ﬁlled with undertones, the sounds of wild things unafraid and
undisturbed. There is an exalta*on to be felt on rocky peaks with far views of
forest-dad hills and peaceful valleys that is emphasized by the busy chickadees
hun*ng bugs on the balsams close by, the fragrance of the hot earth, the sound of
a brook far below. Suddenly one feels the importance of the exquisite perfec*on
of a *ny fern, the ﬂash of a tanager or the contempla*ve assurance of a thrush's
song. These things gave us a sense of permanence, a glimpse into a serene world
that was passed through the bi*ng sculpture of *me and has learned to endure
hardship, s*ll knowing happiness and being able to transmit it to us.


       Sept. 18, 1967
       Dear Mr. AMenborough:
       I had the great pleasure of spending a night last week with my son
       Stephen in the new Congdon Shelter. This is a lovely liMle camp! I
       am more pleased than I can say that the GMC has recognized my
       husband's ac;ve interest and service to the LT by giving his name
       to such a delighnul new shelter. I am sure that many people will
       enjoy it in the years to come.
       Nothing could be a more suitable memorial to Herbert Wheaton
       Congdon than this. I do want to thank all those who had any share
       in the loca;on of the site, and the actual building of the camp. Will
       you please convey my apprecia;on to them.
       With deep gra;tude and all good wishes for the future of the GMC,
       I am
       Sincerely, Helen W. Congdon

<!-- page: 31 -->
Caretaker Impostor Invades Congdon Camp
        LTN November 1986.
       In early August an individual moved into Congdon Camp and began
       collec;ng overnight use fees from unsuspec;ng hikers. The
       impostor, an unsavory character who only spoke to ask for fees,
       made overnight visitors feel very uncomfortable which le> a bad
       impression of the GMC and its Caretaker Program. Legi;mate
       southern Caretakers soon encountered problems collec;ng fees
       from hikers who mysteriously resented the Caretaker program.
       Southern Field Assistant Ray Auger was able to obtain the name
       and address of the impostor and the USFS with the Vermont State
       Police quickly solved the problem. The impostor was arrested on an
       outstanding warrant.

Professor Monroe and a group of workers built a shelter at Cooley Glen in 1918.

bunks. simple utensils, outdoor ﬁreplace, splendid water 150 yds. westerly. [GB
2nd Edi*on 1920] The old shelter is now ruined and provides very liTle protec*on.
A new shelter will be built on or near this loca*on as soon as labor and materials

This shelter with bunks, stove, and table is men*oned in O'Kane, 1926. It had a

LT Mile 137.0 — Division 7 — Elev. 3130 G

Built in 1949, this shelter is an open front, built of peeled logs, with bunks for 6 or

Log structure built in 1949 with bunks for 5 or 6 persons and a spring 600 G south.

Cooper Lodge (3rd)
LT Mile 91.3 — Division 5 — Elev. 3850 G

The Killington Stone Hut was 2nd.

The new Charles P. Cooper Memorial Lodge was erected in 1939 by the Vermont
State Forestry Dept. under the direc*on of Mr. Perry H. Merrill, State Forester, on
land given to the State by Mr. Mor*mer R. Proctor. [GB 12th Edi*on 1940] This
lodge is located 100 G downhill on the LT from the Killington spur junc*on near
sites of the old hotel and the round *n shelter. To the west of the lodge is the
Bucklin Trail. It is a stone and wood construc*on with bunks for 12. It is located on
land given to the state by Mor*mer R. Proctor, former president of the GMC and
later governor of Vermont and named in honor of Charles P. Cooper, president of
the Club when a considerable por*on of the LT was complete. It is the highest
shelter on the LT. There are springs 100 G west on the Bucklin Trail and 325 G
north on the LT. A GMC roving caretaker may be present to help hikers protect the
high-eleva*on ecosystem around Killington Peak as well as manage other
overnight sites in the Coolidge Range. An over-night fee may be charged. It has
been in use from 1939 to present. [GB 24th Edi*on 1996] It was extensively
repaired in 1969 by the Vermont Dept. of Forests and Parks. [GB 22nd Edi*on
1983] The stove has been removed. [LT News, February 1977]

<!-- page: 33 -->
Charles P. Cooper Memorial. Near the cold spring, just under the brow of
Killington, that every climber to the peak will remember with joy and longing, is
the new Cooper Memorial Lodge. It stands about on the site of the hotel that
some forty years ago welcomed visitors ﬂeeing in their horse drawn carriages
from the heat of the valley below. Here too was the steel camp that for many
years withstood the cold and heat as well as the guns of roving sportsmen and the
stone shelter that also provided a sort of haven for the weary hiker.

The new lodge was built by members of the Plymouth CCC camp under the
direc*on of the Vermont Forestry Service in coopera*on with a commiTee of the
club headed by Cola J. Cleveland of Rutland. The land on which the camp stands
has been deeded to the state by Mor*mer R. Proctor of Proctor. The building
opera*on was started in 1938 but had to be discon*nued at the *me of the
hurricane in September of that ye'&' and the work was completed in the summer
and fall of 1939.

The accompanying cut gives a good view of the lodge and its sightly loca*on
above the stunted, wind-blown spruces of the peak. It overlooks the OTer Creek
Valley and in the distance are the Taconics in the west with Pico and lesser peaks
on the main range to the north. The building is about 18 x 20 feet in size. The rear
part is built of stone to the roof and the front half is stone to the boTom of a long
row of windows that extends clear across the front and halfway of the two ends.
Above the windows the structure is of wood. In the interior are tables, seats and
the usual bunks oﬀering sleeping room for eight [LT News, Oct 1939] Dedicated
June 2, 1940. [LT News, May 1940] The Killington Sec*on can no longer take care
of maintenance and is turning care over to the main Club. [LT News, Nov. 1959]
The LT Patrol completed extensive repairs on the roof which was badly roTed,
window frames were rebuilt, and 2 bunks were repaired. Much packing was saved
by the new Killington Basin Char liG. [LT News, August 1960] AGer extensive
repairs in 1960, there was vandalism which required further repairs this year., A
new stove was installed, window replacements (2 new 6 pane sashes and 30
panes of glass) and the roof seams med. Materials for the windows cost $20.77.
The chair liG transporta*on was free. [LT News. Nov 1961] NOTICE - Due to adult
vandalism, all 96 panes of glass have been smashed out We are forced to make
this an open shelter. We plan to clean up the place, replace a missing stove part,

<!-- page: 34 -->
put in new stove pipe, and install screening over the windows to prevent
porcupine entry. [LT News, August 1962]

This framed cabin was built in 1989 by the Laraway Sec*on and named for Robert

Cowles Cove Shelter (1st)
LT Mile 165.3 — Division 9 — Elev. 2520 G

Near the present shelter are the remains of the old Shelter built in 1920, with

Cowles Cove Shelter (2nd)

This log lean-to has space for 8. It was named for Judge Clarence Cowles of

Edi*on 1996] Theodore H. Talbot, NY Sec*on, announced that ground will be
broken for a new open shelter at Cowles Cove in early June 1955. [LT News, May
1955] A new shelter has been completed. It is 3 sided built of peeled logs. It
commands a ﬁne view to the southeast of the Mad Dog Range and the White

Culvers Lake Shelter
On the Appalachian Trail in New Jersey

On November 16, 1959, with 72 Sec*on members present, this new shelter, which
ﬁlls an important gap in the AT shelter system, was dedicated in memory of the
Sec*on's late president, Gren Anderson. The shelter stands in Stokes State Forest
near Culvers Lake. A plaque at the loca*on was unveiled by Mrs. Anderson. It was
built with funds raised by the New York Sec*on. Club members did the building,
led by designer Sam Wilkinson, a trustee, and Harry Smith with the assistance of
many members and took the en*re summer to complete. The shelter is a deluxe
aﬀair with a plank ﬂoor, a built-in table as well as a park-style movable table, rus*c
rain guTers for the front eaves and a stone ﬁreplace. The one-holer features a red
signal that can be operated only from the inside, an inven*on of Harry's. A reliable
spring is about ﬁGy yards away. An actual test on the night before the dedica*on
showed the sleeping capacity to be nine. The shelter was formally turned over to
representa*ves of the Stokes State Forest who will be responsible for its future
maintenance. [LT News, Feb. 1959]

Mile 13.1 — Division 6 — Elev. 2620 G

4th shelter in this loca*on

This shelter is 0.2 mi. down the New Boston Trail. It is a framed lean-to with bunks

Carmel Camp, the last of the steel palaces on the LT, is no more. It gave-way in

An enthusias*c LT hiker, an End-to-Ender, and a prospec*ve Caretaker. This
outstanding young man had deep feelings about the nature of the Trail and the
need for its enlightened stewardship. Dedica*on ceremonies were scheduled for
Oct. 16, 1976. [LTN Nov. 1976]

Dunsmoor Lodge

Referred to as Camp #4 in GB 1st Edi*on 1917. Located 1.2 miles from the Bolton

This lodge was built in 1918 (?) by the newly organized Lake Pleiad Sec*on. This

In June and July of 1911, Cowles and Burt returned to Nebraska Notch and
resumed scou*ng trail over the shoulder of Mount Clark to the summits of Mayo

<!-- page: 37 -->
and Bolton, and down over the forested ridges to the Winooski River. Passing an

along the en*re length of the trail. [Forest & Crag by Laura and Guy Waterman,
1989]

Lodge is in poor condi*on but aﬀords shelter. Owing to objec*on on the part of
the owner of the land, it will not be repaired. At some later date a new lodge will
be located nearer Bolton. [Supplement to GB 6th Edi*on July 1927] E. P. Dickinson
noted in GB 7th Edi*on 1928 that this camp was in poor condi*on. GB 8th Edi*on
1930 also stated that the Lodge was in poor condi*on. The Bolton Lodge built in
1928 and which is nearer Bolton Village will be replacement. Probably not used
aGer 1932.

In 1919 Mr. & Mrs. Ralph Dunsmoor of the Burlington Sec*on gave to the Sec*on
the giG of Dunsmoor Lodge. This structure was located approximately 2 miles
north of the present Bolton Lodge, from He-Who-Remembers (R.B.) we have the
informa*on that this had a unique feature, a front sec*on which liGed up to form
a roof over a pa*o arrangement. By 1923 Mr. Bryant, State Forester, was advising
the Sec*on to relocate the lodge or somehow get it out of the area because it was
a ﬁre-hazard to his lumbering opera*ons. His advice was heeded in due course
and the present Bolton Lodge was built in 1928. [Burlington Sec*on Records 1910
– 1927]

East Shore Shelter (Li>le Rock Pond) (1st)
Mile 14.3 — Division 4 — Elev. 1854 G

The LiTle Rock Pond Ten*ng Area was the 2nd at this loca*on.

This shelter was a framed lean-to with room for 8 and was built by the USFS in
1962. Due to heavy usage and a fragile lake shore environment, it was moved 0.3
mile south on the LT in 1972, reconstructed and renamed Lula Tye Shelter. The
East Shore Shelter was used for 10 years. It was located near the present LiTle
Rock Pond Ten*ng Area and the Caretaker site.

This shelter was without graﬃ* in 1963 when Joanne & I passed by in 1963 on our
End-to-End journey. [Paul Woodward]

<!-- page: 38 -->
A Long Trail Recipe. Take two shelters in the wrong places and add the concern of
the USFS and the GMC about increasingly unsa*sfactory condi*on in the area.
Combine ﬁGeen volunteer high school students and ﬁve leaders. Season liberally
with the experience and know-how of one Sandy Stare, master shelter builder and
prefabricator. Blend together smoothly, and add copious amounts of energy,
enthusiasm, and good supervision. Allow to boil and bubble vigorously for nine
days or so and then witness the happier sight of two shelters in beTer loca*ons.
This is what was cooking at LiTle Rock Pond in late July and early August 1972.

For some *me, it had been apparent that removal of the two shelters to more
suitable sites was a necessary ﬁrst step in restoring the rapidly deteriora*ng
condi*on at the Pond. The Forest Service was willing to sponsor the project and
was anxious to get the job done. The GMC concurred with the need and had the
man who could provide the technical supervision. Manpower and leadership were
available from the Student Conserva*on Associa*on, a nonproﬁt organiza*on
devoted to providing outdoor living experiences and public service conserva*on
projects for high school students.

Under the leadership of Rick and Nancy Meyer and their assistants, SCA members
ﬁrst turned their aTen*on to the island shelter. Under Sandy's watchful eyes, they
cut the shelter into several sec*ons and ﬂoated it across the pond to the outlet
area. Then they carefully transported the sec*ons down the trail to the new site
some distance north of the pond. Next came the job of reassembling the pieces
into the original combina*on. So, well was the en*re job done that not one board
was cracked during the process, and only a very careful study of the shelter can
detect the fact that it had been dissected and put back together again.

Much the same procedure was followed with Lula Tye Shelter. This *me, though,
the sec*on could not be conveniently ﬂoated and each one had to be laboriously
transported to the new site some distance south of the pond. All was completed
when each shelter had toilets in their proper loca*ons, and the old bridge to the
island yielded grudgingly to determined eﬀorts to remove it. Harry Peet used
parts of that bridge to improve the trail along the east side of the pond. The old
sites were thoroughly cleaned up and the new sites were carefully prepared for
the use which they would soon receive. [LTN Nov. 1972]

<!-- page: 39 -->
Ellen Cabin
This ruined cabin below the summit of Ellen was built in 1903. [O'Kane 1926]


Emily Proctor Lodge (2nd)
Mile 5.9 South — The President's Sec*on — Elev. 2600 G

The path then zigzags down steeply, makes a long circuit to the north and then to
the southeast into the valley of the New Haven River, where it reaches Emily

mile from LT.


Emily Proctor Lodge (3rd)
Cost of this lodge was $154. which was a giG of Emily D. Proctor. [LT News, May
1939] It is par*cularly to be regreTed that the lovely Breadloaf Glen where the
new Emily Proctor Lodge was built in the spring of 1938 was in the direct path of
the big storm. The road to South Lincoln was obliterated all the way into the
camp. Across from the site of the camp the torren*al rains started a mud slide
which took not only the trees and other growth, but even the soil, so that what
used to be a beau*ful mossy green diﬀ is now an ugly, raw, rocky ledge. This mass
of earth and trees dammed the brook, and the pent-up waters liGed the camp
from its founda*on and twisted it into a new loca*on. Fortunately, the building
itself was liTle damaged. It has been repaired and the founda*on replaced so that
it can be used un*l some more deﬁnite plan can be made aGer the road is rebuilt.
[LT News, Oct. 1939] The Emily Proctor Trail passes an unmaintained spur leading
leG to the abandoned Emily Proctor Shelter. [GB 15th Edi*on, 1956] This shelter,
long in disuse and abandoned, is to be burned. For some *me, it has been a
hazard in the forest. Paul Newcomb consented to seeing to its disposal. [LT News,
May 1959]

<!-- page: 40 -->
Emily Proctor Shelter (2nd)
Mile 16.8 — Division 7 — Elev. 3460 G

The old Breadloaf Shelter was torn down and an Adirondack type open shelter

This is a log shelter built by the LT Patrol in 1960 and rebuilt in 1983 and has space

July 10, 1964: on top of Breadloaf with thunder all around us we decided to make

[Paul Woodward]

This stone structure, with bunks for 10, is provided with two inside ﬁreplaces.

ﬂoor, and a new stove. [LT News, August 1941] In May the Selectmen of Woodford

French Camp
LT Mile 209.3 — Division 10 — Elev. 1200 G

Through the kindness of Mr. Phillip French of Johnson, who owns this property, a

This frame camp was built in 1931. It was the ﬁrst camp built by the LT Patrol and

June 3, 1972, work trip to French Camp: Day ideal - sunny and pleasant. Group
found French Camp in excellent shape thanks in large measure to Millie and Pat
Yadanza of Sparkill, N.Y., who under the GMC Adopt-a-shelter program have had
FC as their foster child for the last year or so. Only work here was propping up the
slightly *lted outhouse which the Yadanza apparently built. [Bob Hagerman
6/3/72]

Work Ou*ng, Oct. 20, 1973: Teillon and Hagerman met Strobe at Allen S. Hill Stove
Shop in Johnson. Here we picked up a 25 Cushion Quilted box stove. ... Hill had
cleaned up the stove and ﬁlled cracks. ... The group carried everything to the
camp in short or der with their ﬁrst arrival serving to roust out of the sack two
sleeping hikers who had spent the night. Accomplished:

   1) The new stove took the place of 2 other stoves; one was a small cast iron
      stove in serious disrepair which was designed to bum coal and was
      unsuitable in the ﬁrst place. ... The stove rests on concrete blocks which
      were set on two widths of metal rooﬁng. A layer of sand was placed in the

<!-- page: 42 -->
boTom of the stove to protect the boTom and apparently to improve the
      draG. With appropriate ceremony Teillon laid a ﬁre and Hagerman lighted it
      to inaugurate the stove. It worked beau*fully.

   2) Strobel's major project was to construct sashes for windows ...

   3) A piece of metal was shaped and nailed along the boTom of the door to
      cover two large porcupine holes and prevent further damage. A few days
      later 3 Vermont Forests and Parks Dept. employees with Brent Teillon
      completed most of the baTen stripping. The front of the cabin looks good-
      despite its 42 years. French Camp is now in good condi*on, outwardly at
      least. [Bob Hagerman 10/20/73]

Work Trip July 12, 1975: Fred Judd & Lee Haselton applied a coat of roof tar to the
roof and installed some new stovepipe. [Bob Hagerman 7/12/75]


Frizzel Cabin
This cabin was on land purchased by the Na*onal Park Service for the AT corridor.

It is 13 feet square, made en*rely of copper alloy steel. Inside are four bunks, with

Glen Ellen Lodge

This shelter is 0.3 mile down the Barton Trail. It was built in 1933 by the LT Patrol

This shelter was built in 1919 and is provided with comfortable bunks, a stove, and

GMC Camp
The Worcester Sec*on is developing another camp. This camp is located in

at which *me it is hoped that enough interest will have been aroused to build an

1948]

The sec*on feted their past presidents at camp one Sunday in April. 75 members
past and present gathered to honor the leaders of the Club. A social hour and hike
preceded a covered-dish supper aGer which movies of club ac*vi*es were shown.
Each president who aTended gave one or two highlights of his reign. Inside the

1953]

4th shelter near top of Glastenbury Mt.

This log lean-to with space for 12, was completed by the Bennington Sec*on and

Gorham Lodge
Mile 10.6 — Division 9 — Elev. 3400 G

In honor of H. W. Gorham, a member of the New York Sec*on from 1923 un*l his
death in August 1949, his friends are making contribu*ons toward a new cabin or
shelter on Camel's Hump (or, if you prefer, Couching Lion) for which Prof.
Buchanan has selected a new loca*on. farther down the north slope than the
present dilapidated shelters. Bill grew up in the St. Johnsbury region and was
par*cularly fond of this mountain. Such a memorial seems par*cularly ﬁlng for
one whose love for man and earth. and whose apprecia*on of the comfort and
gladness that associa*on with forest and mountain can bring were so great.
Contribu*on for this shelter may be sent to the treasurer of the New York Sec*on:
Ronald Busse, Pearl River, New York, made out to the H. W: Gorham Memorial
Shelter Fund. [LT News, Feb. 1949]

The Lodge is about 112 mile north from the old "Tin Huts; one of which was
dismantled to provide rooﬁng material and stands on the edge of a quick drop oﬀ,
with a magniﬁcent view to the north over the Winooski Valley. Bolton Falls and
the main road near it are clearly visible, as is LiTle River Reservoir. The lodge is
only 500 feet west of the old LT and close to excellent water. The structure is built
of logs, probably the largest ones that have been used in any camp to date, laid

<!-- page: 47 -->
horizontally up to the boToms of the windows and ver*cally above which results
in more eﬃcient use of *mber and saves *me. The lodge is about 15 by 20 G in
size and has a stove brought down from one of the old huts. It is located
wonderfully but is the most inaccessible camp for the Patrol to work on. All
materials that could not be cut at the site or dragged down from the Huts had to
be packed in 2 1/2 mile from Couching Lion Farm. This took *me sweat and
muscle. [LT News, Nov. 1950]

A log cabin with bunks for 12 was built in 1950 by the LT Patrol in memory of H. W.
Gorham with funding from the New York Sec*on. The lodge was extensively
rebuilt in 1981 by the patrol and GMC volunteers. A brook to the east provides
water. A seasonal GMC caretaker educates and assists hikers and maintains the
site and nearby trails. A small overnight use fee is charged. Wood ﬁres are
prohibited. It has been in use from 1950 to present [GB 24th Edi*on 1996]

This lodge has been a source of debate for many years. It is on State property at
high eleva*on on Camel's Hump. The State will have the ﬁnal say as to the des*ny
of the Lodge when they complete the management plan for this area. The lodge is
in poor condi*on and needs repairs. Eight Jogs were replaced and leveled, a new
ﬂoor installed, window frames adjusted, a new door hung and applica*on of
several coats of creosote applied. A picture in the LTN of those working on the
project shows Ben Davis, Chet Buchanan, Dean Tucker, CharloTe Beam, Fuss
Belding, Steve Burns, and Cleo Billings hard at work. This project took ten days.
[LTN November 1981]

The views at night at Gorham Lodge are superb in clear weather. I remember a 2
a.m. walk with the moon and stars ligh*ng my way. The few lights in the valley in
homes and on the highway were just impressive. The mountains to the north were
silhoueTed, giving me a mys*c feeling of grandeur. [Paul Woodward]

I used this shelter with Keith Edson in 1963. It was very buggy, had many

Happy Hill Cabin, (locked), the southernmost cabin of the DOC cabin chain, is
reached at 15.2 m. This enclosed structure shelters six men and is equipped with a
wood stove and cooking utensils. The water source (which may go dry during
summer months) is a small brook 50 G to the west. In case of failure, the spring

<!-- page: 52 -->
men*oned in the descrip*on of the DOC William Tucker Trail may be used. The

President William J. Tucker by Rev. John E. Johnson, class of 1866. The present
