[Dartmouth Ou*ng Club Trail Guide; 1968]

This cabin was removed in 1998. A new shelter will replace it a 1/2 mile to the
southwest.


Hazen's Notch Camp (2nd Shelter in Notch Area)
Mile 5.1 — Division 12 — Elev. 1500 G

The Fred Tucker Shelter was the 1st shelter in the Hazen's Notch area.

This cabin was built in 1948 by the LT Patrol. There are bunks for 8. A tent plajorm
is on the right a short distance from the camp. Water is available at Wade Brook
50 G south. This camp was constructed by Roy Buchanan's LT Patrol. Buchanan
camps have a uniform design and have become a classic symbol of "old Vermont
camps" and that period on the trail. He borrowed the design from logging camps
of the *me. They were easy to construct, used the interior space eﬃciently, and
some wood trap windows could be opened out and used as a table. It has been in
use from 1948 to 1997 when it was dismantled and moved one mile north. on the
LT. [GB 24th Edi*on 1996 & LTN Winter 1997]


Hazen's Notch Camp
Mile 6.1 — Division 12

Thanks to the heroic eﬀorts of the Vermont Youth Conserva*on Corps, GMC
volunteers and staﬀ, Hazen's Notch Camp has a new home. This summer this
historic camp was moved from its site 0.5 miles north of Route 58 to the saddle
between Sugarloaf Mt and Bruce Peak. It now sits 1.6 miles north of the notch.
The former site on Wade Brook was beau*ful: however, several condi*ons made it
unsuitable for use in today's world. Outhouse loca*ons were unsuitable due to

<!-- page: 53 -->
seepage into the brook. Vandalism and trash also made this loca*on near the
highway undesirable. It was a party loca*on.

The GMC decided to move the shelter rather than build a new one to pre9erve a
shelter built by the LT Patrol of the 1930's & 40's; the camp at Hazen's Notch was
by far the best candidate. A GMC ﬁeld team inventoried and evaluated every piece
of wood and determined that the bulk of the building was in excellent shape and
the lumber could be reused. The project was the perfect job for a VYCC wilderness
crew, a group of Vermont youth 16 to 19 years of age who spend a month in the
woods gaining a sense of sa*sfac*on from their labors. Although the 50-year-old
nails in hand-oiled wood and many apertures on the walls made reusing the
materials seem impossible. Jeﬀ Bostwick, a GMC volunteer who reconstructs post-
and-beam barns for a living, said the project would be easy. The job required
careful aTen*on to detail. First the building had to be taken apart without
damaging the wood. The team measured, evaluated, and numbered each piece of
lumber. Then the VYCC piled the lumber into piles by ﬂoor, roof, and walls. The
VYCC, GMC ﬁeld staﬀ, Saint Michael's College outdoor volunteers, Lamoille County
Summer Ac*vity Program, Don Hill, Becca MyeTe, Al Fiebig, Pete Ketcham, and
other volunteers helped with the transporta*on of materials over the mountain to
the new site.

Most of the building was put together with original materials. Only the sills and
the ﬂooring are made from new lumber and a metal roof was installed instead of
a tar roof. A new outhouse was constructed. It is the ﬁrst 'moldering' outhouse on
the LT using red worms to process the waste under the seat. The project took:
2,300 volunteer and staﬀ work: hours to complete. The work: was accomplished
despite the major ﬂood of '97.

This project was the ﬁrst *me an an*quated cabin had been moved to a diﬀerent
loca*on on the LT. By going the extra mile to preserve Hazen's Notch Camp, we
have saved an integral part of the LT system. [Tim Teirney, LTN Winter 1997]

Journey's End Camp
Mile 0.5 Journey's End Trail — Division 12 — Elev. 1515 G

Built in 1931, the framed camp has stove and bunks for 10 or 12, water in brook to

Water is found at a brook 300 G north. It has been in use from 1933 to present
[GB 24th Edi*on 1996] We have a sugges*on for obvia*ng the inconsistency of
naming the camp to be built at the junc*on of the Trail with the Canadian
boundary "Journey's End." That is, to call it "Journey's End" on the south end and
"Journey's Beginning" on the north end. This would have the advantage of being
true on both ends, and the camp would have the dis*nc*on of being the only one
on the Trail. or probably anywhere else, with two perfectly good names. [LT News,
Sept. 1933]

I believe the trees have obscured this view today. The ice storm of 1998 did

The Juggernaut
(Abandoned farm) 1 1/2 miles from West Bridgewater village. [GB 1st Edi*on
1917]

<!-- page: 56 -->
The Juggernaut Trail - "The interest in the projects of the GMC developed in
Woodstock resulted in funds being subscribed to build a trail up Killington Peak
from West Bridgewater, was completed in late August 1913. The trail follows a
wood road to an abandoned lumber camp called "The Juggernaut". The origin of
this unique name is lost unless a high mortality aTended the opera*ons of the
camp. The trail proper starts at the camp and winds up at the eastern slope of
Killington to the summit. 3 miles." [The Making of the Long Trail by Dr. Lewis J.
Paris]


Kid Gore Shelter
Mile 14.2 — Division 2 — Elev. 2800 G

Located on a short loop trail, it is a log lean-to with bunks for 8. It was built in
1971 by the Connec*cut Sec*on volunteers and Camp Najerog alumni and is
named in honor of Harold M. (Kid) Gore, late owner, and operator of Camp
Najerog. Its water source is a spring 10 yds. north of the shelter (unreliable in dry
seasons). In use from 1971 to present. [GB 24th Edi*on 1996] The Connec*cut
Sec*on is in the throes of gelng building materials to the site of the new Shelter
which is being. built at the site of Caughnawaga. Last fall they felled and peeled
the logs for the framework but s*ll need sawed lumber to complete the work. This
site is so remote, much eﬀort has been made to ﬁnd a good route for hauling
materials. On Feb. 7 and March 20, 1971, several members, some with
snowmobiles, assembled at the site of the former town of Somerset 6.5 miles
north of Rte. 9 on the western branch of the Deerﬁeld River. Two aTempts have
not yet yielded the secret best route to haul materials to the site. The best route
may s*ll be locked beneath several feet of snow and s*ll in the hands of Mother
Nature. [LTN, May 1971] A recent addi*on to the shelter system. The Kid Gore
Memorial Shelter located 5.2 miles south of the Arlington-West Wardsboro Road
adjacent to the Caughnawaga Shelter was completed in June 1971. Kid Gore
passed away in 1969, an old friend of the Long Trail. It was his Camp Najerog that
built Caughnawaga Shelter at Glen Haven in 1931 and maintained some ﬁve miles
of trail. His most important contribu*on to the Long Trail was the part he played in
shaping the lives of so many young men. [LTN, August 1971] On Sunday, August
29, 1971, 113 people hiked to the shelter for the oﬃcial dedica*on Program.
Thanks to George Bragdon and Bob Gore for the ﬁne program. To provide easier
access, Alan Mead and Robert ATenborough scouted a side trail from the

<!-- page: 57 -->
Somerset area westward to the LT. This shortcut route is now referred to as the
Nutmeg Trail. Construc*on started on May 15, 1971, and was ﬁnished on June 12,
1971. Although it is an open shelter, it resembles and contains furnishings
normally found in a closed camp: It is a spacious size, 20 X12 feet with sleeping
space for 15. Between the double-deck bunks on each end is a large table which
can seat 8 to 10 people. Ini*al cost was $450. The remaining $191. is to be used
for maintenance. The front faces the east and aﬀords spectacular sunrises and
illumina*ng moonlight in the night. Due to its hilly and rocky loca*on, it does not
have a "Mon*cello" type front lawn. [LTN, Nov. 1975]

On the west side of the summit cone the Club has a shelter situated just above the

Taylor soon began to deplore the limited number of mountains on which good

Round metal huts giving protec*on from hedgehogs, an open camp. Bunks, stove,

A new stone lodge has been erected on Killington Mt. about 1/10 of a mile above

[Supplement to GB 6th Edi*on, July 1927] A caretaker is usually kept here by the

Just north of Black Branch Crossing is Kiln Camp. Bunks in an old charcoal kiln. [GB

This lodge, probably located a short distance north of the lake, was built in 1919

Lake Pleiad Shelter (2nd)
LT Mile131.3 — Division 7 — Elev. 2140 G

This frame shelter maintained by the Bread Loaf Sec*on had bunks for 12. It was

double *er of bunks at each end. [GB 9th Edi*on 1932] It was in use from 1923 to

This shelter is reported in poor condi*on and out of use. [LT News, Feb. 1957] The
complete recondi*oning of this shelter was scheduled by the Special Projects
CommiTee. Thanks for this job goes to George Pearlstein and a group of his Scouts
and Troop CommiTeemen. [LT News, November 1967] Not in GB 24th Edi*on
1996.

On Sept. 22-23, 1967, Troop 45 Granville, N.Y., with 6 adults undertook major
repairs to this shelter. The group arrived just aGer dark on Friday evening.
Materials were stored near road because of fog and unsafe wet foo*ng. The
scouts slept in and around the shelter that night. The next morning, work details
were dispatched. Breakfast was prepared, lumber and materials were transported
to the site, the old ﬂoor and outside sheathing were removed, and reusable
lumber was sorted. The front of the shelter was raised and leveled. Three new
stone piers were built to support the front in its new loca*on. Then the rear was
jacked up and the roTen sill removed. Three new piers were built in the back and
the ﬂoor was leveled. Side piers were installed keeping the ﬂoor at least 6" above
the ground. All old wood was examined for structural soundness. The undersides
were thoroughly creosoted, and a new ﬂooring of 5/4 spruce tongue and groove
planking was nailed into place. The rear window was boarded up and wall
sheathed with spruce shiplap. The bunks were repaired and reinforced. Most of
the sag in the roof was eliminated with the new center post. Most of the work
was done on Saturday as the Scout Troop had to return home. The site needs a
new toilet and some sprucing up. The dump needs to be cleaned and the water
supply should be protected with a cover and a pipe, to be done at a later date.
[George Pearlstein. LT News 1967]

This shelter had to be removed as it was a great party spot near the highway. It
was plagued with vandalism and had major trash problems. The Breadloaf
Sec*on, especially Guy Wallis was responsible for its removal. [LTN Fall 1994]

On an old road near Laraway Mountain was a shack that was not in condi*on for

In the summer of 1930, a party from the University of Vermont headed by

This camp was built on the same site as the burned shelter. A lumber road gave

A private lumber camp on the west shore noted as a ﬁne spot by E.P. Dickinson in

This log structure was built in 1935 with bunks for 6 to 8. The name LiTle Rock

This classic 3-sided shelter was built on the liTle island in the pond. It has a 75-

Keith Edson, Joanne Woodward, and I used this shelter on our very ﬁrst night out
on the Long Trail. It was our ﬁrst backpacking trip ever. We used the oak framed
packs given to us by Joanne's parents, Violet and Whitey Edson. These packs are

when the Island was covered with trees and much vegeta*on. The ledge at the

an airplane ﬂy over twice dumping what looked like a big load of white meat into
the pond. This was before and aGer we sat in the shelter watching a drama*c
thunder and lightning storm pass through the area. There were wildﬂowers in
bloom on the Island. There was a comfortable collec*on of boughs already in
place when we arrived. At that *me. we cooked with a wood ﬁre. The wood was
collected on the hill across the bridge, however. Wood ﬁres were probably the
primary cause for the reloca*on of this beau*ful shelter as this island was a
barren place 10 years later. We were very *red aGer carrying those heavy
uncomfortable packs all day. We slept soundly on the dirt ﬂoor without pads that
night, as I recall. [Paul Woodward]

It was built by the USFS in 1962. This framed lean-to has space for 8. It was moved

Located near the East Shore Shelter which was used from 1962 to 1972. It has tent
plajorms on a rise 100 G east of the pond. This popular pond is one of the most

Long Trail Lodge
LT Mite 100.8 — Division 5 — Elev. 2150 G

<!-- page: 63 -->
In 1923 this beau*ful lodge was built as the GMC clubhouse. Mor*mer R. Proctor
and Mrs. Fletcher D. Proctor provided the funds for building and furnishing the
new home of the Club, and Paul W. Thayer designed the structure. The clubhouse
was so planned as to ﬁt into the rugged surroundings and to u*lize not only na*ve
material but the cliﬀs themselves at the site selected. Logs of yellow birch and of
white birch, with their shining bark untouched, were used for beams and posts
with the building. Lichen-covered and fem-decked rocks became a part of the
walls. A great chimney, res*ng upon a ledge, provided two big stone ﬁreplaces,
one for the lounge and the other for the piazza. Bedrooms were oujiTed with
furniture made of na*ve material. Even the ligh*ng ﬁxtures were fashioned out of
sec*ons of birch limbs, by later ac*on the clubhouse was christened LONG TRAIL
LODGE. The club adopted the policy of welcoming the public at its new home.
Meals are obtainable by any who desire them. Guests introduced by members
may have lodging overnight. Since the building is situated close to the motor
thoroughfare through Sherburne Pass, it is easily accessible and has a host of
visitors. It was destroyed by ﬁre in 1968. [GB 23rd Edi*on 1985] This Lodge had
been sold. [GB 50th Anniversary Edi*on (16th)]

The Green Mountain Club House. The beau*ful building shown on the opposite
page will be opened to members and the public on May 1, 1924. The bedrooms
will be reserved for members and their guests. Guests not accompanied by
members should show leTers from members. Members should be prepared to
show their membership cards or receipts, as they will be en*tled to special rates.
There are nine bed or bunk rooms, each sleeping two to four people. Meals and It
inches will be served to the public as well as members. but why not join the club
and help build trails and popularize the Green Mountains. Write to the Club House
for special circulars. There is a telephone, baths, and all conveniences. This
charming building and its furnishings are a giG to the Club by Mor*mer R. Proctor
and his mother, Mrs. Fletcher D. Proctor. [GB 5th Edi*on 1924] Five small cat ins,
each with living room, bedrooms, and large ﬁreplace, are of the same novel and
aTrac*ve design as the main Lodge and may be rented by the day or week. In
addi*on to these, there are seven small cabins without ﬁreplaces or living rooms,
which are available for overnight guests at a lower price. One of the very
interes*ng features of the Long Trail Lodge is the iden*ﬁca*on and marking of the
plant life, shrubs, and trees in the vicinity of the Lodge by Mr. Maurice Broun, the
Club naturalist, who spends the season at the Lodge and is available to par*es
who wish to study the botany of the Green Mountains. [GB 10th Edi*on 1935]

<!-- page: 64 -->
A club house at Sherburne Pass is now assured. Several acres of land will be leased
from the Vermont Marble Co., and the house will be built next season where the
trail crosses the highway. The trees have been cut where it will stand. The cost will
be paid by private subscrip*on as far as possible, and any balance will be made up
by the general treasury. Mor*mer R. Proctor of Proctor, who has been most
generous in ﬁnancial aid to the club, has started the subscrip*on with $200, and
another friend has subscribed $100. [The Green Mountain News, Dec. 1922] A giG
of $2500. from Mor*mer R. Proctor for a club house was announced. His mother,
Mrs. Fletcher. D. Proctor would donate the furnishings of the house and supply
the water. [The Green Mountain News, April, 1 923] This Clubhouse, which Allen
Chamberlain in an illustrated ar*cle in the Boston Transcript calls a camp deluxe"
and Prof. W. S. Monroe says is "without doubt the most luxurious mountain lodge
in the world." The house was formally opened and dedicated Saturday, Dec. 1,
1923, by a public recep*on. Over 200 were present during the aGernoon and
evening and enjoyed dancing to the music of a ﬁve-piece orchestra, and a general
good *me. A buﬀet lunch was served. [The GM News, Dec. 1923] The principal
topic of discussion before the mee*ng was the management of the new
clubhouse, a unique mountain house, fully furnished and equipped, with
outbuildings, represen*ng an expenditure to date of some $17,500, with much
more remaining to be done before the plant can be considered complete if that
*me ever comes. Mr. &: Mrs. Charles H. Lester of Rutland have been engaged as
managers. This couple have for the past year been in charge of the Knights of
Columbus Home in Rutland. The policy as adopted will be to serve meals to all
who apply. From May 1 to June 15, meals will be served a la carte. From June 15
un*l the close of the season, regular meals will be served three *me a day at $1
per meal. Lodging for guest, whether accompanied by a member or not, will be
$1,50 per night each. The price for members will be $1 per night. [The GM News,
April 1924] A bronze tablet which had been placed in the clubhouse
commemora*ng the fact that the house was the giG of Mor*mer R. Proctor and
the furnishings the giG of his mother, Mrs. Fletcher D. Proctor, has been removed,
in deference to the wishes of Mr. Proctor. One complaint has been heard oGen.
Due to the large numbers of patrons, slow service at meals. Three comfortable
cabins for the help have been built. A garage accommoda*ng 12 cars, icehouse,
woodshed tool house, and an engine house have been built. All is well lighted by
electricity. (The GM News, Dec. 1924] Tablet cost $64. The road from the east to
the clubhouse will be rebuilt this coming summer, and while it is said a passable

<!-- page: 65 -->
way will be kept open during the work, we recommend that during opera*ons the
house be approached from the west, via Rutland. [The GM News, April 1925]
October 24, 1925, a special mee*ng of the trustees was held at the clubhouse.
The name of the clubhouse was changed to the "Long Trail Lodge". [The LT News,
Dec. 1925] Gross receipts of the clubhouse from May 1 to October 15 were
$11,192.35. Expenses $10,791.92. [The LT News, Dec. 1925] The Lodge has had
the most successful season since its opening. Proﬁts were about $1000 for the
season. Extensive changes will be made next year. [LT News, Dec. 1926] The Lodge
operated at a loss in 1927 and 1928. [LT News, Feb. 1929]

The season has opened with a rush, the following large par*es having already
been entertained: the Young Ladies' Academy of Rutland for tea; the Proctor
University Club for a banquet and business mee*ng; the Vermont marble
salesmen; the Eastern New York Eye, Ear, Nose & Throat Specialists, with the
Rutland physicians as guests at a banquet, ﬁlling the Lodge to capacity over night;
the Engineers of the N. Y. Central lines; the Whist Club of Claremont, N.H. for
dinner and overnight; the Arlington Vt., Refrigerator Co's. salesmen and wives,
and other smaller par*es. As Mrs. Bates (the manager) truthfully remarks: "The
delighjul coolness which pervades the Lodge at all *mes makes it a most
aTrac*ve place to visit in the warm summer months." [LT News, Aug. 1929]

The following extensive altera*on and addi*on to Long Trail Lodge have been
decided upon and work has been started on them in accordance with plans drawn
by Paul W. Thayer, the original architect of the Lodge: the kitchen enlarged to
become dining room, capacity of 100, a porch will seat an added 75, a new wing
will contain a serving room, kitchen and cooler, Hillside will be used for a laundry
and bake room, the woodshed will be a dormitory for employees. This is needed
because many patrons were turned away last season. The Trustees feel that no
further addi*ons should be done aGer comple*on of these addi*ons. To make it
s*ll larger would tend to introduce too much of a commercial atmosphere and the
woods charm would be lost. [LT News, Oct. 1929]

Approximately 6800 people had regular meals at the lodge this past season. [LT
News, October 1929] A shortage of steeping quarters is being remedied by
building 3 plain and comfortable cabins to accommodate two persons each at the
cost of $200. each. State Forester Merrill oﬀered to take charge of construc*on.
These will be located in the woods in the direc*on of Pico near the Lodge. [LT

<!-- page: 66 -->
News, June 1932] A discussion about the future of the lodge and the feasibility of
leasing at Special Trustees Mee*ng, Also, winter use. It was decided to open a
couple of cabins to persons whose reliability was known. September 10, 1932. [LT
News, November 1932]

Trustees Mee*ng: The principal business transacted was the approval and signing
of an agreement with the Treadway Service Co., according to which the laTer will
take the possession and management of the Long Trail Lodge for the next ﬁve
years. While there is no guarantee against. losses, which, if any, must be borne by
the owner, the agreement, on the whole, seems advantageous to the Club, and to
oﬀer beTer chances of proﬁt that if managed by the Club, as heretofore. The Club
reserves the privilege of canceling the lease, under certain circumstances. [LT
News, Feb. 1933] The management is sending leTers to those known to be
interested in hun*ng next fall, with a view to remaining open un*l Thanksgiving, if
there is suﬃcient response. [LT News, Sept. 1933] Club advanced for construc*on
of Spruce cabin $500. Gross business was 15% more than in 1936. The Spruce
Cabin was a single room with bath and hot and cold water. From the middle of
July to Labor Day the Lodge was 90% occupied and on weekends 2 to 4 *mes its
capacity were turned away. [LT News, April 1938] In line with the spirit of
promo*ng winter ac*vity on the Long Trail, the thought which prompted the
appointment of a commiTee on winter sports, the Long Trail Lodge opened on
Dec. 10, 1938. The main Lodge will be used for sleeping accommoda*ons for 45
persons. A second-ﬂoor bunk room has been developed. Winter rates, European
plan, are $.75 to $1.25 per day per person; American plan, $3 to $4 per day. [LT
News, Jan. 1939]

At the annual mee*ng the re*ring president, Louis B. Puﬀer, presented a report in
which he brieﬂy reviewed the events of the past six years. . . He discussed the
ﬁnancial condi*on of the club and advised strongly against further expansion at
the Long Trail Lodge. Calling aTen*on to the $25,000 indebtedness of the club,
and to the danger of destroying the unique character of the Lodge, he said: "We
have needed all the accommoda*ons we now have, but we must preserve the
woodland charm of the Lodge selng; if we go further, we will have just another
summer resort." He also suggested that the board of trustees ought to take more
responsibility in the opera*on of the Lodge. [LT News, August 1941] LeTer from
Grover Wright to Mr. Puﬀer: "We are going to open the lodge again for the
1944/45 winter season aGer being separated from the place for over two years."

<!-- page: 67 -->
[LT News, February 1945] Trustees Mee*ng, Oct. 27, 1945. Resolved: That no new
construc*on or other capital investment shall be made at the LT Lodge
Development without the wriTen consent of the Board of Trustees of the GMC.
See lengthy ar*cle in August 1951 LT News.

April 10, 1953, the board of Trustees recommends that the Lodge property be
sold. If the Club decides to dispose of the property, any monies received from
such a sale should be placed in a fund to be known as the Proctor Memorial Fund,
the income from which would be available to the club for carrying out its proper
objec*ves. If the property were sold and the proceeds invested wisely, the Club
could get out of debt and the income would provide suﬃcient funds to take care
of Vermont trails and shelters under our supervision and thus give us beneﬁt from
the giG — beneﬁt which the donors would surely be pleased for us to have. [LT
News, May 1953] On April 24, 1954. an agreement was reached with Grover
Wright for the sale of the Lodge. [LT News, May 1954]

Fire of unknown origin destroyed the sleeping cabin near the Lodge known as
"Undercliﬀ" early in the morning of July 25, 1930. Loss was covered by insurance.
The cabin will be rebuilt. [LT News. August 1930] The new "Undercliﬀ' cabin.
replacing the one destroyed by ﬁre during the summer. is completed. It occupies
the same site, and cost $1,300. Another cabin called the Woodﬁn Camp was also
built near the Lodge, the giG of Mrs. Frank D. White of Rutland in memory of her
mother. Its cost was $750. Each contains two bedrooms and a living room with
stone ﬁreplace and will accommodate 6 & 4 respec*vely. [LT News, October;
1930] The Lodge was not opened in 1942 or 1943 due to restric*ons caused by
the war. An oﬀer was accepted by L. G. Treadway to lease the property from the
Club for a period of years. Mr. Treadway would assume all expenses and pay a
minimal rent for the next 5 years. He did not know when the Lodge would reopen.
The management of the Lodge would be independent of the Club but would
retain ownership out of respect for the donors and the uniqueness of this
aTrac*ve woodland resort. [LT News, April. 1944]

Members of the Killington Sec*on were saddened when they awakened on
November morning to the news that the well-known Long Trail Lodge had burned
down. [LT News, Feb. 1969] Headquarters may move to Mendon. Possibili*es for
loca*on of new oﬃce are 26 State St., Montpelier and the former carriage house
building at the Long Trail Lodge in Mendon. [LTN Nov. 1976]

<!-- page: 68 -->
Lost Pond Shelter
Mile 9.3 — Division 4 — Elev. 2150 G

This shelter, a frame lean-to with room for 8, has a unique background. The giG of
Louis Stare, Jr., then oi Bass River. Mass., it was constructed at Mr. Stare's home on
Cape Cod in.1965. AGer being dismantled into 13 sec*ons, it was trucked 250
miles to USFS Road 10 and hauled to its present site by volunteers using a tractor
and wagon. The lumber. exposed to the salty sea breezes of the Cape, once made
this shelter especially popular with the local porcupine popula*on. The water
supply is a stream in the ravine below the shelter. It has been used from 1966 to
present. [GB 24th Edi*on 1996]

Shelter By the Wagon Load - This all began when Louis J. Stare, Jr., of Bass River,
Mass., saw the new USFS constructed shelter at LiTle Rock Pond in 1963.
Impressed the excellent design of the shelters and the beauty of their wilderness
selngs. Mr. Stare decided that HE would build such a shelter and donate it to the
LT. USFS permission was granted for the erec*on of his shelter on Na*onal Forest
lands. In the mean*me, the LT reloca*on over Baker Peak was completed and the
need become apparent for a new shelter to serve this new trail. So last October,
Mr. Stare and the writer. Ben Rolston visited a suggested site, two miles north of
Baker Peak and 1 1/2 miles south of Big Branch. The site received Mr. Stare's
immediate approval. Over the winter months the shelter was built to USFS
speciﬁca*ons. The disassembled shelter arrived on USFS Road 10 on May 8, 1965.
Along with the truck full of shelter from Cape Cod was a huge Timberjack tractor,
a heavy-duty wagon and 20 GMC volunteers from four states.

Describing the trials and tribula*ons of moving a shelter three miles is like
describing the beau*es of the Grand Canyon to someone who has never been to
Arizona. It just ain't possible! I could tell about fording Big Branch; about the mud;
about the rocks; about the shiGing load; about the con*nuous side-hill lean;
about the unloading and loading and unloading and loading; about the use of the
winch to pull the wagon out of a hole so deep that one volunteer s*ll insists he
heard Chinese voices under the wheels. Our success was due only to the
magniﬁcent skill of Lee Hewes, our tractor operator, and when we had to quit
short of our goal that Saturday aGernoon because of near exhaus*on and a

<!-- page: 69 -->
dwindling fuel supply, Lee kindly oﬀered to return the next morning for the
remainder of the haul to the site. Although Mr. Stare had envisioned his nice new
shelter tumbling down the mountain in splinters, WE REACHED OUR GOAL JUST
BEFORE NOON ON SUNDAY! Due to Mr. Stare's precise construc*on the sec*ons
just fell together, and the shelter was complete by supper*me. When Mr. Stare
applied the hand painted sign "BUILT BY L.J. STARE, JR., 1965", he humbly
remarked that he felt guilty applying his name to the shelter aGer so many
volunteers had worked so hard to make the comple*on possible. The following
Sunday, 17 volunteers put on the ﬁnishing touches and Curt Garﬁeld ceremonially
lit the ﬁrst cooking ﬁre by the use of ﬂint and steel. This epic shelter was
dedicated on May 30th with 31 GMC'ers present. It was christened by John
Rohrbaugh, the new GMC President, with Green Mountain Champagne (pure
water from the stream below the shelter).

Many thanks go out as the work is done. Now we shall reap the beneﬁts. As hikers
prepare their meals at the shelter, breathe the pure Vermont air, enjoy the
wilderness selng, gaze at the stars and the endless depths of the heavens above,
watch the dying embers of the evening campﬁre and restore their strength with
refreshing slumber, we know that Mr. Stare's wishes will have been fulﬁlled. [LT
News, August, 1965]

One year ago, on May 8 and 9, 1965, a LT shelter was trucked from Cape Cod to
Danby, hauled by tractor and wagon up to the LT and assembled on its permanent
site. We named it Lost Pond Shelter. This year, on May 7, an anniversary get-
together was held at the shelter. Louis Stare, the shelter builder and donor, came
from Cape Cod. The Rolstons came from Schenectady. George Pearlstein came
from West Pawlet. Irving White, and Mauri WinTuri came from MassachuseTs
and Bob and Brian ATenborough came from Connec*cut. We came to the shelter
to celebrate its ﬁrst birthday and to reminisce about the trials and tribula*ons of
moving the shelter a year earlier. And to inspect the shelter and do some work on
the founda*on. And to eat lunch.

Following the consump*on of the usual assortment of foods found at any trail
gathering, out came a big cake, topped with one plumber's candle, and the
strained voices of "Happy Birthday, Dear Shelter " echoed up toward Baker Peak
and down toward Big Branch. The ceremonies completed, we set out to ﬁnd Lost
Pond (which had eluded its original discoverer the previous fall). We found it!

<!-- page: 70 -->
Someone suggested renaming the shelter "Found Pond Shelter," but the vote was
NO. Upon inspec*on of Lost Pond, we found it to be of great interest, for it
consists of a small open pool surrounded by a quaking bog. Further inspec*on
found the mat to contain pitcher plants and other bog growth. Since this
rediscovery of Lost Pond, serious considera*on has been given to NOT building
the Lost Pond Trail to the pond. We who have seen the pond feel that the unusual
plant life there should be protected. If, on your ﬁrst or next visit to Lost Pond
Shelter, you do not see a trail sign indica*ng the Lost Pond Trail, you will know
that the decision has been made to let the pond remain inaccessible by trail. You
may then make a nota*on on page 51 of your 1966 Guidebook that the trail to
Lost Pond will not be constructed. Thus, the pond, in name and reality, will remain
LOST-POND. [Ben Rolston. LT News, August 1966]


       WE HATE CREOSOTE
       By Pork U. Pine, Jr.
       Two years ago, from our day;me perch in a big beech tree, we
       watched as a large group of humans, led by a huge chugging
       tractor, invaded our territory. Behind that tractor was a wagon,
       and on that wagon appeared to be some sort of structure,
       somewhat dismantled.
       Those humans put down some very indiges;ble cement blocks, and
       on those blocks, they carefully assembled that structure. We heard
       them call it Lost Pond Shelter. Who cares about Lost Pond? We
       were hungry. Pass the salt, please.
       We waited a week, un;l some more humans returned to put on the
       shingles. A>er all, those same humans wouldn't eat cake un;l the
       icing had been added, would they? Then, assured of our privacy,
       we inves;gated this new delicacy. The brown stain didn't taste too
       bad, neither did the brush that the stain had been put on with.
       Then we sampled the ﬂoor joists and sills. Delicious! They must
       have soaked up some of that salt in the air at Cape Cod. We were
       doing ﬁne on the ﬂoor - even chewed through a couple of the
       ﬂoorboards - when someone discovered that we had been enjoying
       our meals. It was a sad day when two humans from some place

<!-- page: 71 -->
called Schenectady came right into our dining room and nailed
       chicken wire around our dinner. Foiled! But we were determined.
       How about going up into the shelter and sampling its interior?
       Great! Say now, those studs don't taste bad either. And those nice
       big fat center posts! This is real dining.
       Tragedy struck again. We saw him again from our perch. That
       fellow from Schenectady with a big can of something and a big
       brush. Whatever it was, he spread it on thick. Then he departed.
       Perhaps we should inves;gate. Ugh, what horrible tas;ng stuﬀ.
       The label on the can called it creosote. Somehow, we weren't as
       hungry as we had been. Wherever we chewed, we tasted the stuﬀ.
       Wait, let's go up the corner post. Yup, he forgot the ra>ers! They
       tasted as good as the ﬂoor used to. Hooray, back to our meal!
       But we couldn't win. Back came the human, this ;me to nail ;n
       right on the ra>er where we had just dined. And to add more
       creosote. And to creosote the beech tree we had sampled. And to
       creosote the out-house. Creosote, creosote, Bah! Now we suspect
       trickery. We saw him return to put some salt around on some
       nearby stumps. S'funny, he's being nice to us for the change, giving
       us 100 percent pure salt. Must be a catch to it somewhere. Nobody
       has ever been that nice to us. Do you suppose he's trying to bait us,
       only to sneak up some night and assassinate us? We'll fool him -
       we'll move down to Big Branch Shelter. We heard that the USFS
       doesn't use creosote. [LT News. November 1967]

On a spur trail 0.1 mile west. It is a frame lean-to with space for 8. Built by the U.S.

Mile 1.6 — Division 2 — Elev. 2300 G

On a 250 G spur trail, this frame shelter, constructed in 1977, has bunk space for

[GB 24th Edi*on 1996] The site was selected in the summer of 1976. [LT News,
November 1977] A dona*on of $3000 from the family was received to provide for

William D. MacArther Bridge on May 28, 1978; most of the "dedicated" hikers

Minerva Hinchey Shelter (2nd)
Mile 2.6 — Division 5 — Elev. 1530 G

Sunnyside Shelter was 1st.

Located 200 G from the LT. on a grassy wood road, it is a framed structure with
bunk space for 8 and was constructed in 1969 by Louis Stare and members of the
Killington Sec*on. It is named for Minerva Hinchey in tribute to her 22 years of
service as GMC corresponding secretary, 1955 - 1977. Water can be found at a
small spring 150 G south. This shelter replaced the former Sunnyside Camp which
burned down in 1968. It has been in use from 1969 to present. [GB 24th Edi*on
1996] It was named Sunnyside Camp in the GB 20th Edi*on 1971. The Killington
Sec*on has renamed Sunnyside Camp as Minerva Hinchey Shelter. [LTN Feb. 1980]

On Sunday, June 1, 1969, Shirley Strong who is the President of the GMC
christened the new Sunnyside Camp. Sandy Stare who built Lost Pond Shelter has
now moved to Vermont and has now built this camp. The big work party
assembled on May 17 at the site. The lumber was due to arrive at any moment.
The site was cleared, lunches were devoured, and we waited. And waited. But no
lumber. One by one, late in the day, the dejected volunteers retreated. Imagine
Churchill's chagrin when he returned home and found the lumber in his yard!
During the week the misdirected lumber reached its proper des*na*on,
volunteers hauled it into the site with Doc's jeep, and work began. The ﬂooring
and framework had been completed by Friday. Most of the work was completed
the next weekend. On Sunday the 1st of June at 1 pm the ceremony began. Ben
Rolston gave a brief history of the old Camp and told of Sandy's shelter building
experiences. Wes Weston, President of the Killington Sec*on, extended gree*ngs
to all. Shirley Strong, President of the GMC, christened the new Camp. [LT News,
August 1969]


Monroe Lodge
The Camel's Hump Club of Waterbury, which built and has heretofore maintained
three galvanized iron huts on Camel's Hump, has conveyed their *tle to them to

<!-- page: 75 -->
the GMC, and the Club has placed them in charge of the New York sec*on, which
accepted the trust and has had the care of them since June 18. The N. Y. sec*on
plans to build there next year a large lodge of logs, and to furnish necessary
equipment for it, to be known as Monroe Lodge, in honor of Prof: Will S. Monroe,
noted trail maker and president of the N.Y. sec*on. The cost, es*mated at
$1500.00, is to be raised by voluntary contribu*ons, and about two-thirds of the
amount has been subscribed at the present wri*ng. When completed, this will be
a comfortable place for hikers to spend one or more nights, in one of the most
sightly spots on the trail. Food, meals, and blankets will be provided. [The Green
Mountain News, Dec. 1922] It is sure to be built this year, as J. A. Allis of New York,
chairman of the Lodge commiTee, reports that nearly $2000 has been subscribed,
which insures the erec*on of a building amply suﬃcient for all purposes. [The
Green Mountain News, April 1923] The commiTee in charge of the building of this
proposed Lodge on Camel's Hump decided to take more *me for considera*on
and prepara*on and postponed its actual construc*on un*l next year. Plans have
been prepared by Paul W. Thayer of Wallingford, architect of the new Clubhouse,
and show an ar*s*c as well as a comfortable building. The commiTee are striving
to keep the cost down to $2500, which amount they have in hand. Logs will be
used largely in its construc*on, and these will be cut nearby and drawn to the site
of the building on the snow. A year from this *me we hope to chronicle the
comple*on of the Club's second mountain house. The sheet metal huts, which
have heretofore served as camps, will be repaired, and put in good order to be
used as adjuncts to the building. [The GM News, Dec. 1923] No steps have yet
been taken toward building the proposed Monroe Lodge on Camel's Hump, but
we expect this will eventually materialize. [The GM News, April 1924]

Inquiries have been received as to the present status of the Monroe Memorial
Fund; a fund collected for the purpose of building a lodge on Camel's Hump in
honor of Dr. Will Monroe. The proposi*on was to construct the building of logs,
and logs were sold at $10 per log, each to bear the name of the donor. But it was
found that this scheme was imprac*cable on account of the expense involved,
and it was given up. At last accounts the fund amounted to over $2000, remaining
in the custody of J. A. Allis of New York. presumably drawing interest. Any
informa*on rela*ve to this maTer will be gladly received and imparted to our
readers. [LT News, Feb. 1937]

Montclair Glen Lodge (1st)

The ﬁrst lodge at Montclair Glen was built in 1918 by Professor Monroe with his

This is an open board shelter maintained by the GMC. It is provided with four

Roundabout is an open grove of small birches with several plajorms for tents.

experiment proved successful, and it is now planned to protect other shelters on

Built by the Long Trail Patrol in 1959, it is a framed cabin with bunk space for 12. It

and at one *me president. The lodge has bunk space for 6 to 8. A small brook

Sunday, Sept. 19, 1954. Donald Havens conducted the ceremony speaking of Prof.

Mrs. Faulkins spoke on behalf of the family in remembrance of her father. [LT
News, Nov.,1954] This Lodge was ini*ally built as a three-sided shelter and was

When it came *me to put the roof on Puﬀer Camp, corrugated iron rooﬁng was
unavailable so, with considerable misgiving, corrugated aluminum was used. The

Puﬀer Shelter (2nd)
Mile 0.5 — Division 10 — Elev. 3200 G

This shelter was constructed in 1975 by the Burlington Sec*on's "Rat Patrol," with
the assistance of shelter replaces a log cabin which burned in 1974 and is named
in memory of Louis B. Puﬀer, long ac*ve in many capaci*es with the GMC. It has
bunk space fa- 6 to 8. A small stream to the west is an uncertain source of water in
cry seasons. [GB 23rd Edi*on 1985] A tremendous response to an appeal by the
Burlington Sec*on to rebuild the Lodge resulted in its comple*on in about 24
hours. The big problem was carrying the precut materials 3 miles from the road
head to the site. 125 people showed up at the Bolton Valley Corp. parking lot at 8
a.m. on June 14, 1975. Undeterred by ﬂies, mud, humidity, and threat of
thunderstorms, the volunteers carried everything to the site. By 2 p.m. the next
day the job was done. [LTN, August 1975]


Randall Lumber Camp
3.7 Miles south of Bolton RR Sta*on

Buildings in poor condi*on. Stove in ox stable which aﬀords fair protec*on from
rain and cold. [GB 1st Edi*on, 1917] It was known that a logging road ran from
Bolton wen up on the north slope of Camel's Hump and there joined an old trail to
the summit. This route was worked out in July 1911 by President James P. Taylor,
GMC, Prof. R. E. Upson, and Mr. E. H. Allen. Guide boards were put up, the route
blazed and cleared and became the ﬁrst por*on of the Long Trail to be put in
service. The trip begins picturesquely with a ferry boat ride across the Winooski
River opposite Bolton Sta*on, where the trail follows a large brook. The distance
to the Summit is 6 miles, and the easy grade, except, as usual, the last mile. Two
miles below the Summit an abandoned lumber camp oﬀers shelter. ["The Making
of the Long Trail" By Lewis J. Paris, M.D., Chairman State Membership CommiTee
of the Green Mountain Club]


Ri>erbush Lodge (1st)
LT Mile 230.1 — Division 11 — Elev. 1175 G

This lodge was built in 1924. A few minutes beyond Devil's Gulch, the trail reaches
this lodge, a club shelter with bunks for eight. [O'Kane, 1926] This is probably the
same loca*on as the camp that was built in 1933. An open front lodge with stove
and bunks for twenty-four. [GB 6th Edi*on 1924]


Ri>erbush Camp (2nd)
Mile 19.0 — Division 11 — Elev. 1200 G

This camp is a frame cabin with space for 8 and was built in 1933 by the LT Patrol.
A brook 200 G north on the LT furnishes water. It has been in use from 1933 un*l

Edi*on 1996] Removal was completed in June 1999 by ScoT Chris*ansen, Pete

Robert Humes Shelter
See StraTon Pond Shelter


Rolston Rest Shelter
Mile 5.4 — Division 6 — Elev. 2240 G

This framed lean-to with space for B was built by the LT Patrol in 1966. It was
named for Ben Rolston, a former GMC guidebook editor and trail maintainer. A

able to give Ben's name to a shelter. The hew shelter built last summer about a

Roundtop Shelter

Mile 3.6 — Division 11 — Elev. 1650 G

This unique log shelter with space for 10 was built in 1994 by Tod and Wendy
Jenner with the Laraway Sec*on and many other volunteers It is dedicated to
Todd's brother Jeﬀ The logs were cut on Roundtop and skidded to the site the
previous winter Water, obtained from a hand pump is found at the end of a 150 yd
spur trail which leaves the LT a short distance north of the shelter. Pump water like
all water along the LT system should be treated. The quality and quan*ty of water
cannot be guaranteed. In case of pump failure, water might be found in a seep
just beyond the pump It has been in use from 1994 to present. [GB 24th Edi*on
1996]

Between 1991 and 1993, volunteers ﬂagged the trail reloca*on and cleared the
site in 1993 Todd Jenner came along and wanted to give something back to the
mountains He decided to build a shelter in memory of his brother Jeﬀ in the
spring Todd and nephew Ken Jenner with a dozen volunteers cut the trees and
cleared the site In Feb 1994 they skidded the logs 700 to the site by hand and
foot. In June the builders arrived: Todd and Wendy Jenner. Murray Jenner, and
John, Barb and Emily Caviglia. The painstaking task of peeling every log was done,
and the shelter began to take shape It was es*mated that over 80 volunteers
worked on the job from start to ﬁnish [LTN Winter 1994]


Scouts Delight Camp Site
located north of Bloodroot Gap about one half of round trip from WaTer's Farm in

Seth Warner Camp (1st)

Built in 1929 Oﬃcially named the Col. Seth Warner Lodge Stove and bunks for 10.

Beardsley and family, of Springﬁeld, VT (LT News, June 1929]

Seth Warner Camp (2nd)
Mile 3.1 — Division 1 — Elev. 2140 G

Located in an overgrown clearing, this frame structure with bunks for 8 was built

This shelter has been abused in recent years and is in bad condi*on and will be

June 28, 1964. I arrived at camp with a big bucket of water at 1:15 PM Two boys in
their early teens were there hun*ng chipmunks with their new twenty-two riﬂes
They had some trophies. They decided to build a ﬁre in the camp stove when they
got back to camp. I got up from my nap and sat outside in the shade as I know it
would be too hot to rest in the camp. They proceeded to get the cabin on ﬁre. The
kids were very alert and threw a lot of the ﬂaming materials out of the cabin door
and ﬁnished oﬀ the job with my big bucket of water. At sunset Richard and BeTy
Church arrived at camp aGer a very long *ring day. We made room for them and
helped them with a ﬁre as I recall. Water is a valuable commodity [Paul
Woodward]

Mile 2.8 — Division 1 — Elev. 2200 G

On a 300 yd spur trail west from the LT. frame lean to was built in 1965 by

A brook, which may fail in cry seasons, is 150 yds. to the west. A primi*ve ten*ng

Seth Warner Shelter (4th)
Mile 2.8 — Division 1 — Elev. 2200 G

Peter Grasso and Bill MacArthur have decided on a new site for Seth Warner
Shelter, across the brook- from its present loca*on and away from the dirt road
now used by motorcyclists. It is hoped that this move will soon be accomplished

<!-- page: 88 -->
thus comple*ng our reloca*on project. [LTN Feb. 1970 & May 1971l This shelter
was to be moved in early April 1971, but due to heavy snow condi1ions, it stayed

closer to last year's LT reloca*on. On the day of the move the shelter was jacked

Sha>uck Lodge
This shelter, also known as Mould's Shelter, was located 1.5 miles east of the main
LT on a loop trail 1hat descends into a valley known as Chilcoot Pass. [O'Kane,
1926] This lodge was in use from 1913 to 1946.

In 1913 a one-man Sterling Sec*on was opera*ng. His name was Fred W. Mould, a
life*me GMC member who operated a granite business in Morrisville for many
years un*l re*ring in 1930. He played a key role in establishing one of the earliest
shelters on the LT. This Lodge was located about 200 yards east of the present-day
Beaver Meadow Lodge. A gassy, cleared area in the trees s*ll marks the site. In
1913 Mould and others converted what had been an old logging camp used by a
man named ShaTuck. Mould himself is said to have carried in a stove to heat the
structure.

About 1922, Mould, members of his family, and others doubled the size of the
camp. Somewhere along the way, the place, not surprisingly, became known as
Mould's Lodge which appears on the 1953 USGS topographic map of the Hyde
Park Quad. By early 1950 Mould was 81 years old, but he was s*ll an ac*ve hiker
and woodsman. His love of outdoor ac*vity was reﬂected in his oG-repeated
words to friends that "I hope to die on the LT." And just that happened. On June 9,
1950, Mould and another GMC stalwart, Eugene L Bamforth, were hiking to
Beaver Meadow Lodge and apparently planned to con*nue to the top of Sterling
Mountain. Carrying a 40lb pack, the diminu*ve octogenarian (he was ﬁve feet 2
inches tall and weighed only 135lbs) and his companion set out on what was a
scorching hot day. Just short of the lodge Mould sat down to rest. Moments later
he toppled over and was gone. ["History of Sterling Sec*on" by Robert Hagerman]

<!-- page: 89 -->
Camp unlocked, well roofed, accommodates 10 people in emergency: ﬁve single
bunks, ﬁve more can sleep on ﬂoor (no blankets), stove. Mountain brook beside
camp. [GB 1st Edi*on 1917] Mould's Camp no longer exists. [GB 13th Edi*on
1947]


Sherburne Pass Shelter
Across the highway (Rte. 4) from the Clubhouse and a few rods back into the
woods is a shelter built and maintained by the Killington Sec*on for those who
wish to camp. It is provided with bunks, a stove and is supplied with running
water. A small charge is made for its use. Immediately back of the shelter are the
cliﬀs of Deer Leap. [O'Kane 1926]


ShooLng Star Shelter

This log structure was built in 1934 by the LT Patrol and was so named because of

1996] This is an open-front shelter, of peeled logs. with stone ﬁreplace and bunks

Shrewsbury Peak Shelter
2.3 Miles from LT on Black Swamp Trail — Division 5

This shelter was a log lean-to with bunks for 6 to 8 and was built by the CCC. A

Edi*on 1.985]


Skyline Lodge (1st)
Mile 15.1 — Division 7 — Elev. 3320 G

<!-- page: 90 -->
Construc*on of the lodge began in the fall of 1952. The cabin is constructed of
spruce logs, oakum caulked, with inner dimension 14' x 16'. The ﬂooring and
perch are of creosoted rough lumber. It has four windows. a Dutch door, and
asphalt shingled roof. Inside are two double bunks. a loG and a sturdy cast iron
stove. It is equipped with a ﬁre ex*nguisher. [LT News, Nov. 1954] This log
constructed camp was completed in 1954 on a bluﬀ overlooking Skylight Pond, a
small lake only slightly lower than the adjacent summits. It was built by the
Middlebury Mountain Club with a loG and bunk space for about 10 persons.
Water was available at a nearby spring. It is located about the same spot as its
predecessor. This was in use from 1954 to 1986. [GB 23rd Edi*on 1985] As I recall,
it was burned inten*onally in the winter of 1986.


       Whereas, you were born at 4:00 AM in Skyline Cabin on the Long
       Trail in the Green Mountains of Vermont and
       Whereas, you were born by the light of a single ﬂickering candle
       deep in the Wilderness, and at an eleva;on of 3400' at a place
       remote from medical facili;es and
       Whereas, you were born the son of Linda and Jonathan Sewell,
       ardent hikers, and lovers of the mountains of Vermont and
       Whereas, you are the ﬁrst known person to be born on the Long
       Trail and to be exposed, although unknowingly, the youngest age
       of anyone to the warmth and cheerfulness of a lodge on the Long
       Trail and
       Whereas, when you were only a couple of hours old you made your
       ﬁrst adventure onto the Long Trail by being carried on it in a
       portable incubator and
       Whereas, the Green Mountain Club, the organiza;on that built and
       maintains the Long Trail is honored by your historic entry into the
       world. therefore it is
       Resolved, by the Trustees of the Green Mountain Club that you
       shall be an Honorary Junior Member of the Club un;l you are
       twenty-one years of age with all the rights and privileges of
       membership extended to you by the trustees.

Dated October 3, 1970, in Rutland, Vermont



Skyline Lodge (2nd)
Mile 15.1 — Division 7 — Elev. 3320 G

This is a 13' x 14' cedar log cabin with double-decker bunks, inside table, and a
wood stove. It is believed to be the ﬁrst cedar log cabin in the system. Mark Brown
craGed the plans and shared his exper*se in its construc*on. GMC'er Mike
Russom will oversee the construc*on. Russ Eastwood was the eﬃcient low cost
purchasing agent for materials. Al S*les of the Breadloaf Sec*on organized the
work eﬀort The old founda*on was dug out, the site leveled and six holes for piers
dug to bedrock. Holes were drilled into the bed rock for steel pins and then the
piers were poured. Construc*on workers were Ray Auger, Jory Diehlman; Mike
Russom, Doug Thompson, Bob Reardon, Andy Sterns, Andrew Scudder, Ted
Hobart, Russ Eastwood, Becky Eastwood, Doug S*ﬄer, Ben Eastwood, Geoﬀ
Hayter, and Dave Mcevoy. [LTN May 1987]

This is an enclosed shelter with bunk space for 14 and was built by the GMC and
USFS in 1987. It sits on a steep hillside overlooking shallow Skylight Pond. The
pond is not suitable for ﬁshing or swimming. Water is available from a spring 250
G north or from the pond in dry seasons. The area around the pond is very
sensi*ve to resource damage. A caretaker is in seasonal residence to educate
hikers and help them minimize their impact. A fee is charged for overnight use.
There is no ten*ng allowed in the vicinity of the pond, but there is an established
ten*ng area just south on the LT on BaTell Mt. A no-wood ﬁre zone extends 500 G
from the pond to permit the surrounding forest to recover from years of
indiscriminate culng of vegeta*on by campers. [GB 24th Edi*on 1996]


Smith Edwards Camp
Located on the old Johnson-Whiteface Trail about 4.5 miles south of the Falls.

It was always open and showed evidence of hiker and hunter use. In recent years
its loca*on was on a side trail about .4 mile from the LT. There was always a good
store of wood; one room had a kitchen dining area with a stove, the other was the

<!-- page: 92 -->
sleeping area with bunks for 16 persons, maTresses in poor condi*on. Fine
drinking water was located below the outhouse. It was built in 1976. Its condi*on
declined aGer years of abuse and was burned in the winter of 1998.


South Bourn Pond Shelter (2nd)
LT Mile 36.8 — Division 3 — Elev. 2540 G

It was built in 1957 by the Putney Summer Work Camp. Of log construc*on with
an open doorway, this shelter provides bunks for 8. Water is at a spring 250 G east
of the shelter. [GB 18th Edi*on 1966] Named Bourn Pond Shelter. [GB 17th Edi*on
1963] Bourn Pond Camp was ﬁrst shelter in this loca*on.


South Bourn Pond Shelter (3rd)
Mile 7.3 Branch Pond Tr. — Division 3 — Elev. 2540 G

It is a framed lean-to with space for 8, built by the U. S. Forest Service in 1966 on a
bluﬀ overlooking the south shore of Bourn Pond. Water is supplied by the stream
and a boxed spring located on a spur trail 250 G east of the shelter. [GB 24th
Edi*on 1996] This loca*on is very nice. We have camped at this site since we
started backpacking in 1963. As recently as 1995, this area had only minimal

the Pond get heavy use now. It has been used from 1966 to present. [Paul
Woodward]




Spring Lake Camp
LT Mile 78.1 — Division 5 — Elev. 1600 G

The hill overlooking Spring Lake, what a great spot for a shelter! The land in the
open pasture on the northwest side of the pond is owned by Ralph Congdon of
Wallingford, He has given his consent for the building of a camp near the top of
the hill where the trail south enters the woods. There is suﬃcient material at the
abandoned Blue Triangle Camp to build a fair sized, closed camp at this loca*on.
All that needs to be done is tear down the old building, load selected parts on a

<!-- page: 93 -->
truck. transport it to Spring Lake. ﬂoat the lumber across, carry it up the hill a 1/4
mile and build the camp. [LT News, Feb. 1949] On Oct. 1, 1949, Harold ValiqueTe,
Art WestcoT, and Craigue Perkins put in a day at Spring Lake. When they ﬁnished
for the day, the new camp boasted four walls and ridge pole. The sec*ons ﬁTed
together just the way Harold said they would with a bit of patching and piecing.
On a cold windy Oct. 23rd, Harold ValiqueTe, Michael Jennings, and Craigue
Perkins con*nued work. Eleanor Walker, Pearl Nichols, and Vera Perkins hiked in
from the gorge and reached camp in *me to cook dinner for the men. Harold
improvised a table, and dinner was served inside the rooﬂess camp. AGer dinner
Churchill ScoT and Dana Bell showed up. The men kept busy on the heavier work.
and the girls carried boards out of the woods and piled them neatly near the
camp. They also scraped bark from the logs. Progress is being made slowly but
surely. [LT News, Nov. 1949]

On May 28, 1950, 35 members and friends of the Killington, Worcester, and
Burlington Sec*ons spent the day at this camp in Shrewsbury. Although the ou*ng
was not scheduled as a work party, our visitors insisted on pulng in a hard day's
labor on the new camp. Could we refuse? Not so. Led by Jim Green, Roy
Buchanan, Billy Mitchell, and Churchill ScoT, the men carried in logs for the camp
founda*on, jacked up the camp and put the logs in posi*on on top of the storied
piers. The ladies carried stones from the pasture for use in the founda*on. It's
beTer than a diet. girls. At 12:30 p.m. Helen Bailey had the coﬀee ready right on
schedule. Back to work un*l 4:00 p.m. when the out-of-town folks had to head
home. Thanks to all who helped. [LT News, August 1950]

On the ridge west of and overlooking the pond is the new Spring Lake Camp, built
by the Killington Sec*on in 1951 with bunks for 10-12, a unique home-made stove
and cooking utensils. [GB 14th Edi*on 1951] This camp has been dismantled, and
its material is being used for Sunnyside Camp. now under construc*on 112 mile
south. [1955 Supplement to GB 14th Edi*on 1951]

Spring Lake Camp is no more! It has been taken down and moved to a new
loca*on about a half mile south. of its former loca*on. It is the ﬁrst *me that a
landowner has demanded the removal of a shelter because it proved to be a
nuisance. Without warning or discussion, Mr. Congdon ordered the Sec*on to
remove the Camp by March 1, 1954. He told of irrita*ng incidents that had
occurred and he did not want to put up with them any longer. Mr. Wayne Sarcka

<!-- page: 94 -->
of Spring Lake Ranch was contacted, and he gave the Sec*on permission to
reestablish the camp on his land. Sec*on members got into ac*on with the help of
good weather. In four work par*es, the job was done. [LT News, Feb. 1954]


Spruce Ledge Camp
Mile 18.5 — Division 11 — Elev. 1750 G

This shelter is under construc*on during the summer of 1998. This shelter will
open this summer. [Sterling Sec*on NewsleTer. April 1999] This camp will be
oﬃcially and joyously opened to the public by the Laraway and Burlington
Sec*ons at a ceremony on May 1, 1999. This camp is located 0.7 miles south of
RiTerbush Camp which it replaces. [LTN Spring 1999]

On a Sunny May 1st, with wildﬂowers in bloom and clear blue skies, the Laraway
Sec*on and more than 61 people celebrated the opening of this Camp, the
newest shelter on the LT. This Camp is located on a previously undiscovered ridge
that overlooks RiTerbush Pond. It is a simple, well-craGed, beau*fully designed
shelter. On a sunny day, rays of sunshine stream in through skylights and bathe the
wood interior in golden hues... It took 1512 volunteer hours and more than a year
of work to complete... It was dedicated to Don Hill, an extraordinary GMC
volunteer... This loca*on was discovered by Laraway Sec*on Vice President ScoT
Chris*ansen, Randy Meade, Tim Tierney, and Tom Neu in 1997. They were looking
for a place south of Devil's Gulch and a certain distance from neighboring Corliss
Camp. The site is really neat. It is the only view between Laraway and Belvidere.
Located very near the LT and most hikers walked past this loca*on before its
rediscovery... Construc*on of the shelter occurred in 1998. To avoid carrying extra
lumber into the site, volunteers built the shelter oﬀ-site at the home of Cheryl and
Gilbert in Fletcher... The story of the building of Spruce Ledge Camp could read
like the "LiTle Sec*on that Could. With only eighty-two members, this sec*on
built a wonderful camp to shelter LT hikers for decades to come. While they were
making a solid structure, the Laraway Sec*on built *es to new people, community
groups, and other GMC sec*ons. As ScoT reminisced upon ﬁnishing the project,
"The best part of the project was mee*ng new people and making new friends."
See LTN Summer 1999 for the complete story.

Spruce Ledge Camp Volunteers, Laraway Sec*on, GMC, Thanks for all the hard

Naming Spruce Ledge Camp - The sec*on considered several names for their new

Spruce Peak Shelter
Mile 14.7 — Division 3 — Elev. 220 G

This enclosed structure, with bunk space for 16, was built in 1983 by the

Stem Cabin
A Burlington Sec*on cabin in Ranch Camp Valley, Stowe is open to all GMC

Sterling Pond Lodge (1st)

Sterling Pond Lodge (2nd)
LT Mile 199.1 — Division 10 — Elev. 3030 G

The LT Patrol rebuilt this shelter in summer of 1939. [LT News, Sept.,1938] The

[GB 19th Edi*on 1968] Green Mountain Trail Camp Bums at Sterling Pond
Morrisville, July 6. Word was received this morning that Sterling Pond Camp on

<!-- page: 97 -->
the LT burned to the ground late yesterday. The ﬁre was discovered by 2

Sterling Pond Lodge (3rd)
LT Mile 199.4 — Division 10 — Elev. 3030 G

This log structure is in a very scenic loca*on on the top of a low cliﬀ with Sterling

burned in 1950, was built the same year by the LT Patrol for the Sterling Sec*on.
There are bunks for about 8. Drinking water is available at springs 300 G east via

— The major accomplishment was the rebuilding of this Lodge which burned
down last spring. It was started during the lntersec*onal and took three weekends
and many hours to complete the new lean-to. A cookout for twenty people was
held to celebrate the replacement.

In August, the boys went into Sterling Pond Lodge where lumber and supplies
were ﬂoated across the Pond prac*cally to the door of the Lodge. The roof and
bunks were repaired, some ver*cal logs were replaced in front and shuTers were
made for the front windows instead of glass. These are made so they swing in and
are mounted ver*cally, each window having two, one on each side to eliminate
the heaviness of one big shuTer. The dump was moved, and the cabin creosoted
inside and out, so we will see how the Lodge will survive another season. [Jack
Harrington. LT News, November 1967]


Sterling Pond Shelter (4th)

This framed shelter was built by the Montpelier Sec*on in 1972 and has space for

'Head Trail. A GMC caretaker is on duty during the summer to assist and educate

hikers and maintain the site and nearby trails. A fee is charged for overnight use.
Small wood ﬁres are permiTed. It has been in use from 1972 to present. On
Friday, Sept. 1, 1972, the Madonna Mountain Corp. loaded two of their
bombardiers with lumber and other building materials purchased by the GMC and
proceeded up the slopes of Sterling Mountain. Six volunteers rode up with them
and transported the material by hand from the top of the ski liG to the
construc*on site which is the same spot where the old Lodge sat. Two volunteers
baby sat the materials to protect them from the porkies and people. On Saturday,
eight volunteers from the lntersec*onal worked on the stone piles, the ﬂoor, and
carried the rest of the materials to the site. On Sunday, 10 volunteers. including
Roy Buchanan. who was to be the advisor, installed the uprights and back wall. All
the burned logs were cut up for ﬁrewood. Bunks, roof, and table were completed,
and on Sept 23rd and 24th, the ﬁnishing touches completed, aGer which the
group enjoyed a hot dog roast and beau*ful full moon. [LTN Nov. 1972]

signs, cleaned out the cabin, threw out the broken stove, put screening in the

The problem of repairing or rebuilding the camp has preoccupied the BraTleboro

Ta_ Lodge
Mile 10.7 — Division 10 — Elev. 3650 G

TaG Lodge is the oldest Long Trail shelter. Built in 1920, it was the giG of Elihu B.

TaG Lodge is a very heavily used structure. Although its oﬃcial capacity is 32, Ben
Davis has seen over 60 people in residence overnight. Numbers in the 40's and
50's are not uncommon. This puts tremendous stress on the shelter, its water
supply, and its outhouse. And overnight use is only part of the problem. TaG is a
popular stop-over point for day hikers on the way from Smuggler's Notch to the
Chin. TaG has a full-*me caretaker during the summer months and into the fall,
but evidence suggests that winter use takes a heavy toll on the shelter since hikers

<!-- page: 108 -->
and skiers visit the shelter without GMC supervision. [Ridge Lines; vol 23, No 4;
Mar 1994]

This lodge is perched on a shelf below the Chin with a view east. This log shelter,

Sec*on. it was a giG of Judge Elihu B. TaG of Burlington. In 1996 a large corps of

caretaker is in residence during the hiking season to assist, educate hikers, and

has been in use from 1920 to present, [GB 24th Edi*on 1996]

In April of 1918, Theron Dean suggested that the Burlington Sec*on build another
cabin or two on its sec*on of trail in an*cipa*on of the crowds of people who will
come from NY City and Boston to get away from war worries. This plea was
answered in 1919 with a giG from Elihu B. TaG, a local lawyer and judge, for a
lodge to be built before 1920. Judge Cowles and others had scouted the area for a
possible loca*on on such a lodge. At that *me there was a logging road up the
shoulder making access for building there much easier that it is now. Materials
were dragged up by teams instead of on human backs. So, TaG Lodge came into
being and was dedicated on schedule in 1920. Many blankets were purchased for
it, plus a few tents, dishes, and other comforts being added as the years went by.
It was voted in 1920 that this luxurious accommoda*on should have a caretaker.
Judge Cowles, E. H. Smith, and J.P. Taylor were the commiTee to aTend to this.
Judge Cowles put in a short s*nt as caretaker himself. In 1923 TaG Lodge brought
in $167.95 in fees. The caretaker was paid $132.50 for the season plus $74.38 in
supplies to see him through. By 1926 over 1500 people had signed the register.
[Burlington Sec*on Records, 1910 - 1927]

It was a substan*al log cabin and is equipped for the accommoda*on of about
thirty overnight guests. There are comfortable bunks, blankets to rent and a stove
for the use of trampers. [O'Kane, 1926]

Judge Cowles remained an ac*ve hiker and trail worker long aGer his 1910-11
work on the Long Trail, but for the most part he chose to concentrate on
Mansﬁeld, rather than extending the LT farther north or south. He built at least

<!-- page: 109 -->
ﬁve other trails on the mountain as well as its most prominent hikers’ shelter, TaG
Lodge. [Forest & Crag by Laura & Guy Waterman, 1989] About 800 registered at
