and 8 foreign countries. [The LT News, Dec. 1925]

[REMINISCENCES ABOUT ROY. BUCHANAN AND THE LONG TRAIL by Chet
Buchanan] While I was on the LT- Patrol gang (in the 1932-to-1936 *me span) one
of our jobs was to replace all the logs in the north end of TaG Lodge. The LTP at
that *me consisted of Dad, Joe Joslin, and me. The procedure we used was ﬁrst to
remove the 3 boTom logs. Then we replaced the one at the boTom of the
opening and removed the one at the top. That way, we chased the opening up to
the peak of the roof where it disappeared.

We cut appropriated trees up the slope from TaG, peeled the bark oﬀ and slid
them down to the lodge. Some parts of the slope were steep enough so the logs
would slide on their own. We'd run ahead of and to one side of them holding
ropes *ed to the chains around the log. That way, we could steer it and, we
hoped, arrest it, if it decided to head directly for the lodge. One *me, Joe fell in
front of a log, rolled onto his back, planted both feet on the end of the log and it
pushed him a short distance un*l it stopped.

It was at TaG that we encountered Thelma Bonney and Erna Clayton. They were
hiking a sec*on of the LT, some each summer. We ran into them on one or two
other occasions when we were building or repairing cabins, but I don't remember
where. At TaG, they stayed an extra couple of days and helped by chinking the
cracks between the logs with sphagnum moss. I think we ate somewhat beTer
when they were with us. I seem to remember blueberry pies Erna was a die**an.


       THE RESCUE OF A SYMBOL
       by Larry Dean
       Hovering far up under the northeast shadow of Mt. Mansﬁeld's
       Chin is one of the GMC's oldest shelters. This cabin, built of logs
       from those very steeps, cut and rolled into place by the sweat of
       men, has a>er the storms of twenty-one years begun to show age.

<!-- page: 110 -->
Some half dozen years ago the LT Patrol performed an emergency
opera;on and checked a fatal "skin disease," removing bark from
the logs and replacing badly roMed and crumbling ;mber. But years
of heavy snows had begun to force the structure to its knees. This
summer a gang from Burlington got into a huddle, scratched their
heads, rolled up their sleeves, spit on their hands, and went to
work. Jacks cable, dills, hammers, and steel pegs were packed up
over the Sunset Ridge trail from the Halfway House. The old cabin
was pulled back to a standing posi;on and anchored to the ledges
of the mountain, to stay un;l the mountain should move. Caretaker
Daan Zwick packed rolls of rooﬁng across the ridge of Mansﬁeld,
from the Hotel and down Profanity, on some trips carrying 120
pounds to the load.
It took seven week-end trips to slap on the rooﬁng, lay a metal
ridge cap, put in a stove pipe chimney, cut a new window space in
the logs on the east wall, and install three window sashes. Daan
gave the cabin a creosote lo;oning and chinked up the sides. Now
the once ailing old Ta> Lodge stands up strong a>er passing under
the healing hands of Daan and Pierre Zwick, Elton Beal, Lee Brown,
Den Douglas, Ken Fisher, Jerry Grow, Profs. Buchanan and Puﬀer,
Larry Dean, and others.
Ta> lodge has oﬀered shelter to many hikers in the past and, it is
hoped, will to many more in years to come. Above all, Ta> lodge
stands as a symbol of the thought and unselﬁsh energy exerted by
such men as Judge C. P. Cowles, who made 27 trips up to the site
when the lodge was being built, and by those other workers during
the 31 year of Club existence who have made the trails and shelters
possible to trail lovers in and out of the state. One has but to watch
those men and women at work, to work with them to realize the
full value of the Long Trail.
May the big rescues along the Long Trail in the future ever be one
of volunteer coopera;on. May we never become dependent on
other agencies to do the work. May rt always be said, "It was
wrought in mountain pioneer spirit and maintained in the spirit" -
the spirit-of which Ta> Lodge is a true symbol. [LT News, Nov. 1941]

<!-- page: 111 -->
This lodge was extensively repaired by the LT Patrol in 1962. The boTom front log
was replaced. The front part of the roof leaked and was repaired with rolled
rooﬁng. [LT New, Nov. 1962] The Patrol spent 3 weeks at this lodge. Four logs put
in on the south end, a new roof, ﬂoor replaced, bunks repaired, and a new
outhouse completed. The Mountain Co. hauled our supplies up to the top of the
new liG. [LT News, November 1968] On March 16, 1969, we had the rededica*on
of the lodge which was aTended by a large group. The over-nighters dug about 9
feet of snow for water in the brook which wasn't there for some reason. The
Lodge had snow completely covering the upstairs window on the north side. [LT
News, May 1969].


       TaA Lodge – A RestoraCon in The GMC TradiCon
       In the summer of 1930, an eight-year-old boy, his sister, and father
       climbed the Halfway House Trail up the western side of Mt.
       Mansﬁeld. Once on the mountain's ridge, they walked north to
       Lake of the Clouds. "There, my father put us to bed under an
       overhanging rock, and he went down to Ta> Lodge to play cards
       with the caretaker," said Daan Zwick, remembering that trip of 60
       years ago. Ta> Lodge, in 1930, was just 2 years older than Zwick.
       "The lodge fee was one of the reasons my dad had us camping
       out," Zwick said. "I think he wanted to give us that outdoor
       experience, but I think he also was avoiding the fee," for staying
       overnight. By 1940, it cost $.50 to sleep in one of the 32 bunk
       spaces in Ta> Lodge.
       Zwick and his father, Pierre, and his sister, Hudde, climbed to the
       summit of Mt. Mansﬁeld many ;mes over the next 8 years. and
       only occasionally did they sleep at Ta>. But the boy who so rarely
       got to stay in the largest and oldest-remaining LT shelter was soon
       to become caretaker of the lodge and eventually to fund its
       reconstruc;on. ... Ta> Lodge is part of the cultural history
       surrounding the na;onal conserva;on movement that was
       underway in the early twen;eth century, according to Cur;s
       Johnson, a historian in the Division of Historic Preserva;on and a
       GMC member. ... Historic Preserva;on asked only that the general

<!-- page: 112 -->
appearance of the lodge be preserved (in the restora;on) given the
diﬃcul;es of working on a site three-quarters of the way up the
state's highest mountain .... Although the current reconstruc;on of
Ta> is the most complete the lodge has seen, it is not the ﬁrst.
Varying historical accounts note at least three signiﬁcant eﬀorts to
repair the cabin, as logs roMed and the structure seMled in 1934,
just fourteen years a>er the lodge was built, several logs were
replaced ScaMered in among the oﬃcial repair projects were at
least a few improvements made by caretakers ... By 1941 ... snow
build-up behind the cabin had pushed the base out a liMle bit
Burlington Sec;on members aMached cables to the lodge and to
the rock behind it and "pulled the cabin up straight again." ... They
also replaced the roof.
Another signiﬁcant repair job took place in 1961, when the Long
Trail Patrol removed a roMen log from the south end of the lodge.
The toll road proved invaluable during these projects ... Without
the road or the type of generous assistance the Vermont Na;onal
Guard provided this year with helicopters, horses would have had
to pull the logs and lumber up the mountain All the smaller
supplies and tools would have been packed up by volunteers ... To
prepare for the helicopters to crop the materials. a volunteer crew
cleared trees from in front of the lodge. ... Even with the help of the
Na;onal Guard and power tools such as chain saws and drills, work
condi;ons at the site are rough. It quickly becomes clear that
although (Fred) Gilbert, who has twenty-ﬁve years of-experience in
construc;on, contracted with the GMC to rebuild the lodge, he is
doing the work out of his dedica;on to the Club. Gilbert is a lifelong
GMC member, a Long Trail end-to-end hiker. and an ac;ve GMC
volunteer. He and (John) BenneM live on site in tents ﬁve days a
week, including every weekend. They work regardless of the bugs,
the sun, or, this summer, the cold. Rainy days conﬁne them to their
tents, and sunny days sentence them to workdays of as many as
sixteen hours.
Once the supplies were on-site, Gilbert. Bennet. and several crews
of volunteers spent a few days gathering the strewn lumber and
other materials and organizing the site. Then Gilbert and BenneM

<!-- page: 113 -->
tore out the lodge's bunks and ﬂoor. They also found a ;me capsule
concealed below the ﬂoor. Once the ﬂoor was gone, they cut the
walls out with a chain saw and installed the truss system to
support the roof. In order to keep the roof, which is no longer
connected to anything, from blowing into the mountainside, they
;ed it down with ropes running from each corner out to four of the
larger trees around the cabin. Then they started hauling rocks and
water to build the stone founda;on .... The new founda;on, which
required sixty bags of mortar, will mean an extra step of two up to
enter the cabin.
... reconstruc;on is on schedule ... In addi;on to the long days
Gilbert and BenneM work, as many as ﬁ>een volunteers help each
weekend, while others help during the week A new ;me capsule
will be placed in Ta>, and GMC is seeking ideas for what to put in
the capsule. Please contact Dana Baron, Fred Gilbert, or Len
Carpenter with sugges;ons. [The en;re ar;cle may be found in the
LT News. Fall 1996]



Change Of Plans for TaA Lodge ReconstrucCon
A shi> of gears in planning for the reconstruc;on of Ta> lodge
became necessary a>er a mee;ng between representa;ves of the
GMC and the State Historical Preserva;on Division. Ta> Lodge,
located near the chin of Mt Mansﬁeld, was slated for replacement
in the summer of 1995. A design was nearing comple;on, and we
were beginning to gear up for the dismantling of the old shelter
when the Historical Preserva;on Division requested a mee;ng. Ta>
is on the State Register of Historic Sites and as such comes under
the jurisdic;on of the Historical Preserva;on Division.
Lars Notzojorns. Brian Fitzgerald. and I (Dana Baron) met with Curt
Johnson of the Historical Preserva;on Division on December 9
(1994). Curt pointed out that. ideally, the Historical Preserva;on
Division would like to see a complete restora;on of Ta>. However,
given resource constraints and the inaccessibility of the shelter.
they recognized that this would be very diﬃcult for the GMC.

<!-- page: 114 -->
Instead, we were given two op;ons for- work on Ta> that the
Historical Preserva;on Division would support.
Op;on one is a refurbishment of the exis;ng shelter, replacing a
minimum of worn components. Op;on two is a reconstruc;on of
the shelter in which the Lodge would be dismantled and rebuilt
using as much of the old material as possible. The Ta> lodge
CommiMee had considered both of these op;ons previously but
eventually decided to proceed with a complete replacement of the
shelter. Based on the outcome of the December 9 mee;ng, this
decision must now be revisited... [Ridge Lines: Vol 24. no 3; January
1995] [More informa;on on Ta> Lodge renova;on may be found in
issues of Ridges Lines, the Burlington Sec;on newsleMer, which are
available in the UVM library.]


TaA Lodge Complete!
A>er three years of planning and a summer of hard work,
volunteers and staﬀ completed the reconstruc;on of Ta> lodge on
Sept. 5; 1996 Construc;on supervisor Fred Gilbert and his assistant
John Bennet, who lived on Mt. Mansﬁeld for the summer, have
packed up their tools and personal gear and headed down the
mountain for the last ;me.
A complete renova;on gave Ta> Lodge - the oldest, largest, and
most heavily used of LT shelters - a new lease on life. Workers
replaced the logs and other materials and installed a new, green
enamel metal roof. A new interior layout features stronger bunks, a
larger table, more headroom, and a built-in closet for the
caretaker's supplies and equipment. Volunteers also built a new
outhouse with scrap lumber le> over from the reconstruc;on. Trail
reloca;ons for the LT and Profanity Trail now route traﬃc around
the shelter to take pressure oﬀ the site.
Our thanks to the many people who contributed to the
reconstruc;on eﬀort. Former caretaker and long-;me GMCer, Daan
Zwick, ﬁnanced the project; Bill Sayre donated the logs: Fred and
John worked full-;me on construc;on; the Vermont Army Na;onal

<!-- page: 115 -->
Guard and the Mount Mansﬁeld Co. helped with transporta;on of
       material, supplies, and people; and many volunteers worked all
       summer to make the project a success.
       On October 6, a beau;ful fall day, 50 people hiked up to Ta> to
       dedicate the new shelter. A>er the dedica;on, the Club recognized
       the many people involved with the renova;on at a small gathering
       at the GMC headquarters.
       Although Ta> is spiﬀy and new, when one sits on the ledge in front
       of the shelter and looks back at the venerable lodge nestled below
       the Chin, there can be no mistake that this is s;ll Ta> Lodge.
       Somehow, with all the newness. the essen;al character of Ta> has
       been preserved. As the shelter grows old and seMles into the
       mountain, the history of Ta> Lodge will con;nue for another 75
       years, and more. [LTN Winter 1996]

Taylor Lodge (1st)
LT Mile 187.8 — Division 10 — Elev. 1850 G

<!-- page: 116 -->
First known as Nebraska Notch Lodge.

By the summer of 1925 need was felt for a lodge in the vicinity of Nebraska Notch.
The Club had been using the Trout Club facili*es. The Trout Club was willing to
donate the land but s*pulated that an open shelter be built. The Sec*on wanted a
closed lodge, so the project was put on hold due to lack of funds and the Trout
Club's s*pula*on. Then the Main Club came through with the oﬀer of $1000 from
the Judge Hazelton Fund for the building of a lodge. It was voted to start building
the lodge in Nebraska Notch and the cost of the structure was not to exceed $750.
Just how the objec*ons of the Trout Club were overcome is not clear from the
records, but the lodge referred to as Nebraska Notch Lodge and later named
Taylor Lodge was· built for $150. less than authorized. [Burlington Sec*on Records
1910-1927]

This lodge, named for the founder of the GMC, was built by the Burlington Sec*on
in 1926 under the skillful direc*on of Ernest E. Smith and Dr. E. G. Twitchell. It is
built of peeled logs and located about 1/4 mile east of the summit of Nebraska
Notch. It is a closed cabin with stove, simple cooking utensils and bunks for 16. Its
eleva*on is about 1900 feet and just back of it is a good lookout to the east over
Lake Mansﬁeld. [Supplement to the GB 6th Edi*on July 1927] This lodge was
burned in the winter of 1951. [GB 20th Edi*on 1971] In Jan. 1951 a group of hikers
who had planned to spend the night there discovered that rt had been burned to
the ground. The ruins were covered with snow. We assume that the destruc*on
was due to carelessness and not to malice, but no evidence has yet been
uncovered as to who the arsonous apes were who set it aﬁre. An ape according to
the lexicon of the Burlington Sec*on is an individual who should not be allowed
loose in the woods without a keeper. The bright spot to this tragedy is that the
Sec*on carried ﬁre insurance for the rebuilding. [LT News, Feb. 1951]


Taylor Lodge (2nd)
Mile 7.5 — Division 10 — Elev. 1850 G

This large cabin is named aGer James P. Taylor, founder of the GMC. It was built in
1951 by members of the Burlington Sec*on under the direc*on of Prof. Buchanan
and replaces a similar structure burned the previous winter. It has bunks for about
12 and ﬂoor space for more, water is permanent, though somewhat remote,

<!-- page: 117 -->
being obtained from the brook, 0.2 mile down the lake Mansﬁeld Trail. [GB 20th
Edi*on 1971]

October 29, 1951. 74 hikers aTended the dedica*on and cabin-shower part at the
GMC's Taylor Lodge. Arriving about midday, they had lunch and inspected the
cabin. All agreed that it was ﬁlng memorial to the memory of the founder, the
late James P. Taylor. [LT News, Nov. 1951]

July 2, 1973, our en*re family was hiking over Mansﬁeld and Bolton. We stayed at
this lodge for the night. The children were especially good. Bobby fell out of the
loG at Taylor in his sleep at 3 am. and got a couple of bruises. He took the window
oﬀ its hinges. The lodge was about full, and we all got an early wake up. [Paul
Woodward]

Taylor Lodge, destroyed once by ﬁre, has burned again. The lodge, located at the
east end of Nebraska Notch south of Mt. Mansﬁeld, burned to the ground Sunday
morning, Sept. 25, 1977. The ﬁre was discovered by an uniden*ﬁed hiker who
no*ﬁed personnel at the Lake Mansﬁeld Trout Club, 1.5 miles away. The Stowe
Fire Dept. was called in but arrived too late to save the building and could only
prevent the blaze from spreading to nearby woods. Cause of the ﬁre is unknown
but GMC State Ranger-Naturalist Cecilia Elwert on Mt. Mansﬁeld speculated that a
ﬁre in the stove at the lodge had been leG untended. by a depar*ng hiker, with an
escaping spark or ember subsequently star*ng the blaze. It was insured and will
be rebuilt. [LT News, Nov. 1977]

23 GMC followers braved the elements and took the long uphill climb to Taylor
Lodge Sunday, February 1, for their second Oyster Stew supper. An advanced party
packed in the makings of the supper. Water and ﬁrewood were brought in. Groups
came in on snowshoes and skis. Although the weather was cold, the snow made it
a pleasant trip in. Beverly Thorpe prepared the oyster stew supper. Pies were
donated and the feast was excellent. The thermometer registered 8 degrees
below when the group departed with ﬂashlights in hand about 8. p.m. [LT News,
May 1953]

       A Tailor-Made Lodge

<!-- page: 119 -->
by Marion Holmes


I slept in a lean-to at 10 below.
And now there are lots of things I know.
That I would do if I were the guys.
To design New Taylor and surprise
The hardy souls who’ll be sleeping there.
And breathing in that good fresh air.


Standard equipment ought to be.
A snowblower, for you see,
Bedding down on a layer of ice
is deﬁnitely not what you'd call nice.
Since electric power's a dirty word
And solar heat is much of heard,
A solar panel in the roof, converted,
Could heat electric blankets in all packs inserted.
Since rumored it is. there are those who snore,
You could vent the sound through roof or door.
With masks and pipes, like on a plane.
Perhaps not needed during a hard rain.
Since a lean-to hazard rather abrupt
Is bumping the head when straightening up,
The roof should be of rubber sponge,

<!-- page: 120 -->
No danger then from a sudden lunge.
It seems so foolish to have the ﬁre.
And picnic table outside the lair,
A conversa;on pit with sunken coals
Would beMer achieve fellowship goals.
Since backpacking means to carry weight
And pounds of food will alter gait,
A concession is the thing that's needed,
Howard Johnson's sign would sure be heeded.
And ﬁnally, who wants to climb.
To reach the goal on heights sublime?
An escalator is the needed thing,
All hikers it would to Taylor bring.
You have to feel sorry for J.P.T.
That he these marvels couldn't see,
But I believe-that he'd approve-
Or would he cry, "Remove! Remove!"?



Remarks at DedicaCon of 3rd Taylor Lodge
Sept. 1978
by Jan AbboM, Burlington Sec;on President


Fi>y-two years ago, when the 1st lodge on this site was
constructed, the soaring stock market, ﬂaming youth, and the jazz

<!-- page: 121 -->
age were in the news and "Silent Cal" Coolidge was President of
the U.S. The Burlington Sec;on of the GMC, more liberated than
the rest of the country, had already had two Women presidents.
One of them, Mrs. Laura Cowles, was the ﬁrst woman to snowshoe
to the top of Mount-Mansﬁeld.
Although Judge Cowles had wriMen a leMer to James P. Taylor as
early as 1910, the year the club was founded, concerning building
the trail from Mansﬁeld to Bolton, with a branch running down to
the Mansﬁeld Trout Club, The LT as we know it was not yet
complete when the ﬁrst Taylor Lodge was built. During the next
decade, when Roy Buchanan and others were pushing through to
the Canadian border, Larry and Alberta Dean arrived in Vermont
from Manchester, N. H., got oﬀ the train in Essex Junc;on, and took
the trolley past the busy cavalry post at Fort Ethan Allen to
Burlington, where room and meals were $4 to $5 a week at the
boarding houses, Main Street was paved with cobblestones and
a>er leaving a restaurant near where Preston's is now located,
Larry's young bride looked around and said. "But where is the main
street of the city?!" The Prohibi;on rum runners were busy and
Burlingtonians made home brew in BaMery Park.
Back in Nebraska Notch, The Burlington Sec;on had built a
comfortable closed lodge of peeled logs, with bunks and
maMresses for about 16 campers. However, Larry Dean recalls that
the outhouse was on the primi;ve side, and only semi-private.
Unlike the 1951 and 1978 structures, the ﬁrst Taylor Lodge faced
away from the outlook.
Rod Rice must have been well regarded by older club members, as
he served as Taylor caretaker when he was only ﬁ>een years old. At
one point during that summer, he saw no hikers for four days, and
two weeks passed with no overnight campers. By this ;me, 1939,
the old county road through Nebraska Notch had been abandoned
for over ﬁ>y years, but Rod brought his bicycle into Taylor from
Stevensville, and somehow managed to roll and carry it down to
the Trout Club, to use in visi;ng rela;ves in Waitsﬁeld.

<!-- page: 122 -->
A LTN of 1941, under the heading "The Art of Cookery," men;oned
the anonymous hiker who put a can of baked beans in the Taylor
stove pipe oven, without punching a hole in the can: According to
the ar;cle, the result "conﬁrming the well-known theory that beans
contain a large supply of stored energy!" Rod Rice recalls a similar
incident, this ;me with a can of corn that took oﬀ like a rocket,
trailing a stream of corn.
World War II came and went, and about the ;me of the Marshall
Plan and the Berlin Airli>. two high school students with more local
interests joined the GMC — Donald Remick and Beverly Thorpe.
LiMle did they realize, 30 years ago, that their names would come
to be more closely associated with Taylor Lodge than that of
anyone other than the GMC founder himself.
Nor did anyone suspect then that a group of boys burning
maMresses to keep themselves warm would burn Taylor Lodge to
the ground during the winter of 1950-51. The following summer, LT
Patrol head Roy Buchanan, aided by Don Havens, Jake Bailey, and
others, built the lodge that most of us remember so fondly. They
also built the famous outhouse "Doris", named a>er a woman
hiker who showed great concern over the non-existent facili;es.
Rod Rice, who by then had abandoned his bicycle and advanced to
more powerful means of transporta;on, remembers awakening
the sleeping construc;on crew one night when he drove his
motorcycle into their campsite near the Trout Club. A>er an
amazingly rapid transforma;on to the more sober role of family
man, he arrived at the dedica;on of the second Taylor Lodge with
young Rod, then 11 months old, on his back, a feat which any of
you who have seen young Rod today will be sure that his father
hasn't aMempted in recent years.
Harold Collins, at that ;me the Burlington Sec;on President,
remembers that day, October 29, 1951, as cold, miserable. dark,
cloudy, foggy and rainy-Don and Bev Remick remember it as a
lovely fall week-end-but in any case, 74 hikers turned out, and
showered the new cabin with gi>s of frying pans, pots, axes, saws,
pails, brooms, dustpans, and just about everything needed to keep
house.

<!-- page: 123 -->
Soon a>er the dedica;on, the Burlington Sec;on Ou;ng CommiMee
was trying to think of something new and diﬀerent. Don Remick, by
then a senior at UVM, suggested doing something crazy-'1ike
ea;ng oysters on the mountain." He and Bev Thorpe took it on as a
challenge, and the ﬁrst Taylor Lodge Oyster Stew was held in
February of 1952, with 30 hikers aMending. The price of oysters
was about $7.50 a gallon then. and about $16.00 a gallon last
year. With Don. or alone, during his service years. Bev Remick ran
the oyster stew ou;ng for 25 winters. No fall Ou;ng CommiMee
mee;ng during that era was held without a calendar showing the
phases of the moon. so that the Oyster Stew Supper return trip
could be made in moonlight-it it didn't happen to be snowing,
slee;ng, or pouring rain-one year the temperature was 14 below
zero. Home-baked pies, tenderly packed in, were the tradi;onal
dessert, and keeping them level in a pack was always a challenge.
Oakum was o>en packed in too, since ﬁlling the chinks between the
logs ranked with wood-cuzng and hiking as an a>ernoon pas;me
while Bev cooked the stew.
By 1963, the Oyster Stew Supper had become so popular that it
was diﬃcult to ﬁnd a place to stand, let alone sit, with 62 persons
in the cabin, so if you feel a liMle crowded at the moment, be glad
we're not all in there trying to keep warm! By this ;me, the GMC
was holding week-long summer intersec;onals at scout camps, and
it some;mes felt as though we were conduc;ng winter ones those
nights at Taylor.
As the years passed, and the GMC adapted to the changes of the
70's, caretakers were once again hired to protect our major lodges
and to educate the vast numbers of new hikers on the trails. This
year we have been fortunate to have with us Ms. Ann Pesiri, who
has been a great help with the construc;on as well as undertaking
the usual caretaker du;es. Unfortunately, we s;ll have careless
hikers, and last fall the Head Ranger on Mt. Mansﬁeld, Cecilia
Elwert, had the sad duty of informing the Burlington Sec;on that
another Taylor Lodge had burned.
Most of you here don't have to be told what eﬀort has gone into
the construc;on of the building that we shall shortly be dedica;ng

<!-- page: 124 -->
to Jack lance, Dot Myer, and everyone who assisted with the
       construc;on and the planning of the carry-in day put incredible
       hours into this project carrying on the volunteer tradi;on of the
       GMC. We are more sophis;cated in 1978-the latest surveying
       equipment was used to level the site, instead of Roy Buchanan's
       cake ;n ﬁlled with water-the lodge has been designed to meet the
       restric;ons of a new genera;on of hikers, and to last 50 years, at
       least, but if we could wish any one thing for Taylor Number 3, it

Thendara Camp
The New York Sec*on maintained a camp for many years on Lake Tiora* in Bear
Mountain-Harriman Sec*on of the Palisades Interstate Park. The deal to take over
Thendara was closed in the fall of 1922 and the camp opened with Bill and Beth
Barclay as chairmen of the commiTee. The camp was supported by raising the
sec*on dues by one dollar a year, with the understanding that the money would
be returned to the treasury when it was no longer needed. This was done in 1928
when for a *me the camp was self-suppor*ng. Thendara proved an ideal
recrea*on spot. Community cooking was ins*tuted in 1927. In winter, water had
to be carried by pail from the pump house, a test of manhood being to carry two
pailsful the 700 feet uphill without stopping or sliding in the snow. Developments
in the 28 years of camp life include enlargement of the main cabin, installa*on of
a motorized pump and electric lights, canned gas with a modern stove and the
building of an incinerator by club members The 2 canoes increased to 6 and an
aluminum rowboat. Bathing facili*es have been installed Many trails have been

<!-- page: 125 -->
constructed to nearby points of interest such as the old Hogen Camp Mine Today
Thendara is a part of the club life of every ac*ve member whether he chooses to
skate, ski, swim, canoe, sail, aTend the Bird census or just loaf [LT News Feb.
1950] On October 1. 1972, on a beau*ful sunny day, the NY Sec*on celebrated its
50th Anniversary at Camp Thendara. A great turnout of 134 members and guests
gathered [LTN Nov 1972]

This camp is located near Harmon Hill in the Bennington sec*on and takes the

On Sunday. October 5, 1969, 17 GMCer's and guests hiked into the Shelter for the

Vondell Shelter
Mile 9.5 Lye Brook Trail — Division 3 — Elev. 2555 G

It is located about 0.2 mile from LT at Willis Ross Clearing on south shore of

Interna*onal Paper Co. The shelter is named in honor of John H. Vondell, an ac*ve
member of the Worcester Sec*on for many years, a former trustee and president

a short distance on the trail. [GB 24th Edi*on 1996]

Surprise shelter - For more than 30 years on the weekend aGer Mother's Day 1he
Worcester Sec*on has assembled faithfully on the StraTon Road and were
dispatched to various sec*on trails for the annual spring work party. This year the
trail workers cleared trail toward StraTon Pond. There were a few blowdowns as
usual. Along the Pond's south shore to Bigelow Shelter all was well. But as they
progressed westward toward the pond's outlet, they stopped short in amazement.
There, before their very eyes, was a new shelter! WHODUNIT?? Finally, the secret
was revealed. Interna*onal Paper Company had done it. What'll we name it?
Perhaps Surprise Shelter would be appropriate. With such heavy use of the
StraTon Pond area, this new shelter will be a valuable addi*on to our LT system.
Here is a splendid example of landowner interest and coopera*on. So, to
Interna*onal Paper Company, we say "Thank You'. [LT News, August 1967]

In May 1967 the Interna*onal Paper Co. built a new shelter for LT hikers at
StraTon Pond. Since the passing of our beloved John Vondell in September of

<!-- page: 131 -->
1967, we have oGen thought of the many years that John went on Worcester
Sec*on work trips to StraTon Pond with George Story and Louis Bigelow, Now,
with John absent from our midst, it is a ﬁlng tribute that his memory be
perpetuated with a shelter named in his honor. The Interna*onal Paper Co. has
kindly given us permission to name their new shelter as Vondell Shelter. Thus, as
Louis Bigelow and John Vondell met side by side at StraTon Pond, now Bigelow
Shelter and Vondell Shelter rest almost side by side at the pond which they loved
so well. We mow that John would have been pleased to have this shelter named
in his honor, at one of his favorite places on his beloved LT. And we are all richer
because John walked there with us. [LT News, August 1968] This shelter will be
removed StraTon Pond's shoreline aGer the new StraTon Pond Shelter has been
completed in September 1999. [LTN Fall 1999]

Nov.6 1971, Sterling Sec Members; Brent Teillon, Bob Hagerman, Trip Leaders,

William MyeTe Scoutmaster, Caroll Brothers. Wade Chaﬀee. Randy Williams.

Boards 12' long, 6" wide and a full-inch thick. were carried up the Whiteface Trail
to the shelter, also the sheets of metal rooﬁng were drug up ... the wire screening
serving as a ﬂoor was removed so the accumulated porcupine dung could be
removed... a reasonably level ﬂoor was laid over the screen. The boards had been
creosoted. The new ﬂoor greatly improves the shelter ... Hagerman, meanwhile

ﬁreplace rebuilt ...successful trip due in large measure to the help of the Boy
Scouts. All who par*cipated felt a 9"eat sense of accomplishment having put

Work Trip June 14, 1975: in generally good condi*on but sill logs are deteriora*ng
so their replacement will be needed in the not distant future. [Bob Hagerman
6/14/75]

Work Trip Aug. 6, 1977: Objec*ve of this trip was replacement of the old,
galvanized metal root. The roof was a patchwork aTar to begin with, assembled
from pieces of rooﬁng which probably had been on the shelter which had
preceded this one. Con*nuing complaints had been received in recent years about
the roof leaking. ... Two steps preceded the work trip itself: (1) GMC's LT Patrol

<!-- page: 135 -->
replaced deteriorated sill logs. (2) Teillon had driven all the supplies by 4-wheel-
drive vehicle to within 1/4 mile. … he got hung up on a rock and to unload all the
materials there, then walk out to get help ... two ladders were built. weather-
wise, prospects were dim ... the old roof removal started at 8:45 a.m. because of
rain. Because of the rain, 4 leaks in the roof gave very wet evidence of the need
for a new root. The old roof was removed, and new boards nailed in place. An
under layer of 15lb felt rooﬁng was laid in place and nailed down. At that point it
stated to rain ... at that point the under layer made the roof waterproof for at
least a *me. AH went home. Plans for ﬁnishing were indeﬁnite ... we thought one
or another of the GMC caretakers at shelters on the LT in the Mt Mansﬁeld area
would be willing to complete the job. [Bob Hagerman 8/6/77]

During the 1996 spring walk-through, Sterling Sec*on workers cut and peeled a
large diameter spruce to replace both sill logs. With the help of GMC Field
Assistant Tim Tierney and the Sterling Pond caretaker Michael Moss, Sterling
Sec*on stalwarts Bob Lindemann and Smith Edwards hauled up jacks and
replaced the north side before the rest of the workers arrived. The rest of the
crew helped ﬁnish the south side. Bob's work with the chain saw produced some
horizontal cuts on the new piece, which made it easier to get the right ﬁt under
the exis*ng logs of the sidewall. AGer *dying up on the roof and founda*on, we
wrapped up the shelter work before mid-aGernoon. [LTN Spring 1997]

A closed log siding cabin with shingle roof, stove, and bunks for 12. [GB 14th

Wiley Lodge
Mile 14.9 — Division 8 — Elev. 1620 G

Early in 1962 the Girl Scout Roundup CommiTee suggested to the Burlington
Sec*on that the Club build a Pioneer Cabin as an exhibit for the BuTon Bay
Roundup. The Sec*on decided to build a typical Long Trail Cabin. The cabin would
be a 12' x 14' structure with double bunks in each rear caner which would sleep a
total of 8 persons. Between the bunks is a table 2 1/2 feet wide with a seat on
each side which is really an extension of the boTom bunks. The front of the camp
was standing room with only a stove to get in the way. Window openings were on
all sides with wooden shuTers. The price of the logs was going to be $220 per
thousand feet un*l the commiTee found a sawmill who would cut thick slabs
which could be debarked by the ladies for only $75 per thousand feet. Now, as
Camp Emeritus. is wai*ng pa*ently at BuTon Bay un*l its builders recover from
their ski fever, when they will move it to some favorable loca*on on the Long Trail
where it can spend its declining years in peace if not in plenty. Thanks go out to
the Burlington Sec*on for their hard work of building a Long Trail Cabin right in
the Village Green at BuTon Bay for the Girl Scout Roundup. [Roy Buchanan, LT
News, Feb. 1963]

Originally constructed as a GMC exhibit at the. BuTon Bay Girl Scout Roundup in
1962, it was reassembled at its present site by the LT Patrol in 1964. Named for
William 0. Wiley of the NY Sec*on, who furnished funds for the original Wiley
Lodge (once Buchanan Lodge), this frame cabin has bunks for 10 to 12. An

<!-- page: 137 -->
adjacent small brook furnished water. A GMC Caretaker was in residence during

The Patrol gang started work on June 15, 1964. 60 man loads of material including
12' and 14' lumber was packed in. Don Wallace led the backpackers while Roy
Buchanan watched as he ﬁn95 watching much easier. The lodge was completed.
except for the stove on July 13, 1964. [LT News, August. 1964]

On Nov 2, 1989, two employees of the State Forest. Parks and Recrea*on Dept.
discovered the burning remains. It is suspected that the ﬁre was caused by an

LT Mile 40.7 — Division 3 — Elev. 2470 G

It is located facing the pond and is a frame structure with bunks for about 12. A
spring is 30 G to the north. The camp was erected by the Worcester Sec*on in
1929 and named aGer Willis Ross of Rutland, through whose eﬀorts many miles of
Long Trail were built. [GB 20th Edi*on] This structure, located on StraTon Pond,
was built by a party of 23 member of the Worcester Sec*on in ﬁve days, August
17-21, 1929, last, under the leadership of George F.E. Storey and Louis L. Bigelow.
The lumber was contributed by W. A. Savage of Randolph, Vt., and all of the labor
was donated. Much diﬃculty was experienced in gelng the material in. It had to
be carried by manpower over an old swampy corduroy road, a most arduous
undertaking. Fortunately, a troop of Boy Scouts happened along just at the right
*me and were of great assistance in the transporta*on work. It is delighjully
located in the midst of spruce trees. with no other sign of human habita*on
visible on the banks of the pond with the beau*ful hills rising on each side. [LT
News, Oct. 1929] It is located at the east end of the Pond on the largest and most

<!-- page: 138 -->
beau*ful body of water on the LT. It is one of the most aTrac*ve camp sites on the
whole trail. [GB 9th Edi*on 1932] No caretaker at StraTon Pond. [1955
Supplement to GB 14th Edi*on 1951] It is an open fronted shelter as pictured in
the LT News, Dec. 1929. It was reported that this camp had been occupied for
weeks by a party of young men, causing hikers to pass along or sleep outside. And
they may be there yet, as we have not heard that they have been ousted. If trail
camps are to be u*lized as summer homes, the Club will have to devise some way
of remedying the evil. [LT News, Sept. 1933]

Announcing the installa*on of a new stove, complete with oven, at Willis Ross
Camp! Weight, around 130 lbs. Arrived via rubber-*red wheelbarrow on Saturday
evening, November 8, 1941, and was connected early Sunday morning. The
installers. several Worcester Sec*on members and friends. tried out the oven by
baking gingerbread. and tes*fy that stove and gingerbread were an unqualiﬁed
success. Two gallons of sweet cider were used to toast the arrival of the stove at
camp aGer a somewhat arduous trip in from the Grout Job.

Camp was leaking badly so last week I got a gang together and we lugged in
material and put a new roof on. dug out a decayed back sill. pulng stones under
the sleeper, straightened up and fastened front doors, repaired broken bunk,
replaced stones under front which had been taken out and used for ﬁreplace in
front of camp and generally policed the place. It is now in good condi*on for
winter. There was evidence that someone occupied the camp as a home last
winter. Cost us $35. to repair Camp but it was good investment. [LT News, Nov.
1945]

Weekend at Willis Ross Camp. October 12. 1946. The Worcester Sec*on enjoyed
its ﬁrst oﬃcial ou*ng since the war at StraTon Pond. The nine members who leG
Worcester on Friday night arrived at the Grout Job about 11. o'clock. A full moon
lighted the way into camp. Although the night was warm. bent and broken trees
on the trail gave evidence of an early fall snowstorm. AGer a short night's sleep, all
were up early for a look at the weather and a breakfast of pancakes and bacon.

The red foliage had fallen, but the yellow and bronze leaves of the beeches gave
the eﬀect of sunlight. Several climbed the mountain and encountered more and
more! fog the higher they went A light rain was falling on the way back to camp.
The wind and rain that evening did not dampen anyone's spirits, for it was cry

<!-- page: 139 -->
inside and there was much good fun. When bed*me came, those who had
intended to sleep at the annex decided not to venture outside; so, all sixteen

Ba>ell Hotels
In 1899 Colonel Joseph BaTell built a buckboard road to the summit of Mt.
Abraham and a trail along the skyline of Lincoln Mountain. He built a lodge below
the summit of Abraham where he entertained many guests. He built another
lodge on the northern end of the mountain four years later. Although his ridge line
trail predated the Long Trail. It is probably the same trail that exists today along
much of the Lincoln Range and one of the earliest sec*ons of the present-day LT.
Col. BaTell's hotels probably were some of the ﬁrst LT overnight accommoda*ons.

<!-- page: 142 -->
This hotel was located 100 G leG on an old road from Mile 6.7 of Division 8 in the

Beane Farm
At Mile 3.9 Division 8 aGer passing Birch Glen Camp. the Beane Trail con*nues via

The newer members of our Club are acquainted only with the name of Mrs. Frank
Beane who lived in Hanksville and was known to LT hikers for many years. Mrs.
Beane had a great part in the early history of our Club. Our ﬁrst Guidebook,
published in 1917, told hikers that from Birch Glen a lumber road would take them
to the farmhouse of Frank Beane, where bread and simple supplies induc*ng
maple sugar could be obtained, also lodging for two or three persons at
reasonable rates. Mrs. Beane later leG the farm and operated the Hanksville store,
selling food supplies to hikers, providing mail service. and oﬀering meals and
lodging. She con*nued in later years aGer Frank passed, giving up the store and
con*nued to live in the adjacent house.

On one of our visits to Mrs. Beane, she told us that she oGen regreTed not having
kept a logbook. of the names of hundreds of hikers whom she had entertained
over more than four decades. It would have been a valuable record for Club
history. An ailing foot that would not heal regrejully b-ought Mrs. Beane to a
Waterbury nursing home last year. Her spirits were high, and she was going to get
beTer so that she could return to her beloved Hanksville home. A visit to her
would always cheer up the visitor. She possessed a strong religious faith which
gave her courage in those dark days. As outrageous as she was, the inevitable
conquered. Mrs. Beane passed on at the age of 88 just before Christmas 1966. She
was a true pioneer of our Club, and those of us who knew her so will miss her
deeply. We know that she walks the Trail of Gold in the Great Beyond. She will
oGen look. down upon her beloved Camel's Hump under whose shadows she
spent so many happy years. By Ben Rolston. [LT News, November 1967]

Mount Mansﬁeld Summit House
Mile 8.7 — Division 10 — Elev. 3849 G

In 1856 the ﬁrst overnight lodgings were set up for trampers on Mt. Mansﬁeld.
The date has been deﬁnitely ﬁxed by Judge Clarence P. Cowles of Burlington
through a statement secured by him in April 1924. from Cyrus Mead, of Underhill.
The shelter was a tent, and it was set up by David N. Shaw and George Downing
near the spot where the hotel now stands. The ﬂoorboards for the tent were
carried up by a man and a boy. The man carried four at a *me and the boy two.
They made one trip a day and their charge for the service was $.25 per board in
the same year in which the tent was set up, a trail was deﬁnitely cleared up the
mountain. At the sugges*on of W. H. H. Bingham. a prominent ci*zen of Vermont
and a resident of Stowe, construc*on was begun in 1858 on a carriage road
planned to approach the mountain from the easterly side. The road was built as
far as a point under the steeper part of the summit ridge and trail was built from
there to the top. Lumber was hauled over the road and the trail. and a small hotel
was erected in 1858 at the place where the tent had stood. An addi*on to the
hotel was built the following year as was another in 1868. Both road and hotel
were purchased by the present Mt. Mansﬁeld Hotel Company in 1919. Rebuilding
of the en*re road was at once begun in order to make it passable for automobiles.
This undertaking was completed two years later. In 1923 the hotel was rebuilt and
again enlarged. It is a comfortable and well- managed inn and is altogether an
interes*ng place in which to stay. [O'Kane, 1926] Hikers have always been
welcomed at Summit House which provides 2 bunk rooms for 18, as well as
individual rooms. It is open from last week in June to Labor Day. [GB 50th
Anniversary Edi*on 1960]

One of the last of the famous mountain hotels, this hotel burned down in 1964
aGer more than 100 years of opera*on. The only hotel which was directly on the
Long Trail. An adver*sement in GB 3rd Edi*on 1921 reads, "The Mount Mansﬁeld
Hotel Co. cordially invites all trampers to make the Mount Mansﬁeld Hotel their
headquarters while visi*ng this sec*on of the trail. You will receive a hearty
welcome. Open from June1st to October1st on the Long Trail. C.R. Riley, Manager.
Hotel Rates Four dollars and ﬁGy cents and up per day. American plan. Weekly
rates on applica*on. Bunk room for hikers, if desired, at reduced rates. [GB 5th
Edi*on 1924] The last season open as a hotel was 1959. [LT News, May 1960] This
is now occupied by the Summit Sta*on, Television Sta*on WVNY, and FM Radio

<!-- page: 150 -->
Sta*on WEZF. Here, during the summer season, snacks may be purchased, and a
telephone is available. [GB 22nd Edi*on 19/33]

<!-- page: 154 -->
General Informa:on for GMC System
Overnight Campsites:
On March 4, 1910, TaG sent a call to eighteen prominent ci*zens to form a "Green
Mountain Club". The stated purpose was to make the Vermont mountains play a
larger part in the life of the people to awaken an interest in the mountains of
Vermont, to encourage mountain climbing, to make trails, build shelter, and aid in
the prepara*on of maps and guidebooks." [Forest & Crag by Laura & Guy
Waterman, 1989]

Under Buchanan, the LT Patrol not only maintained whatever trail the sec*on
volunteers couldn't handle but did most of the major construc*on work as well.
The crowning achievement of the early years of the LT Patrol was the ﬁlling in of
the chain of shelters and lodges, so that from MassachuseTs to Canada, hikers
were guaranteed a roof over their heads for the night at intervals of never more
than 8 miles. The characteris*c Buchanan camp was a small, enclosed cabin with
bunks, shuTered windows, a single table with benches and a wood stove. Though
the LT Patrol worked on all parts of the trail. most of its early camp construc*on
was north of Mansﬁeld, where the gaps were greatest when Buchanan came on
the scene: French and Barrows were built in 1931, Parker in 1932, Journey's End
and RiTerbush in 1933, Shoo*ng Star in 1934, and numerous replacements and
reconstruc*on over the subsequent years. Butler Lodge, on the southwest slope
of Mansﬁeld, was the largest of the early Buchanan works and the one of which
he was most proud. When he re*red in 1967, it was es*mated that under his
direc*on and personal involvement, the LT Patrol had built 37 of the camps then
in use. All built to such exac*ng speciﬁca*ons as "to within a foot-and-a-half" and
large enough for six hikers or six porcupines, but not six hikers and six porcupines.
[Forest & Crag by Laura & Guy Waterman, 1989]

The commiTee on requirements for Long Trail buildings reported by W.M. Fay that
the types of such buildings seem to fall into three general classiﬁca*ons, viz.,
shelters, camps and lodges which are deﬁned as follows: A shelter is a rough, open
face building of the Adirondack type, of dimensions as stated, to accommodate 6
people at most. It has no stove or other equipment but has an open ﬁreplace in
front for cooking and heat. The shelter is recommended for an overﬂow near

<!-- page: 155 -->
much used camps and lodges, also where the terrain is unusually rough and the
distance between two of the present sta*ons seems long, and to accommodate
those who want to sleep in closer contact with the out-of-doors.

A camp is larger than a shelter and more preten*ous. It is of the closed-open type
with windows and has wooden shuTers by adjus*ng which it can be used either
closed or open. It should have a stove, table, benches, and a wooden ﬂoor. A
lodge is a larger and more luxurious building. It is most useful in those parts of the
Trail that are popular and easily accessible by motor. It should accommodate at
least 16 persons, and should be equipped with stove, table, benches, cooking
utensils, dishes, etc., oGen with a ﬁreplace, plenty of windows, and in some cases
screened. It is recommended that before the Club of any sec*on constructs either
type of building, speciﬁca*ons thereof be ﬁrst approved by the trustees Cf a
commiTee of them. Also, that no buildings will be maintained by the Club Cf
included in the guidebook unless oﬃcially accepted by the trustees. The wriTen
report included diagrams and eleva*ons of a shelter and a camp. [LT News April
1934]

With the Trail completed, the Club con*nued an established program of building
shelters. Prime mover in this eﬀect was Professor Roy O. Buchanan of the
University of Vermont in Burlington. In 1931 the Club's Board of Trustees
authorized forma*on of a salaried crew to work on the Trail each summer.
Buchanan was the Long Trail Patrol's ﬁrst leader. Each year he assembled a group
of students and worked with them on basic trail maintenance, repairs to exis*ng
shelters and construc*on of new ones. During the next ten years the LTP, Sec*ons,
and others built or rebuilt 29 shelters and lodges. Shelter construc*on and
reconstruc*on resumed at a somewhat reduced rate through the 1950's, then
accelerated in the 1960's. S*mula*ng this ac*vity in part was recogni*on of a
factor which the Club has been dealing with ever since - signiﬁcant growth in the
numbers of hikers using the LT system. From 1966 to 1975, responding to the
impact of the increased trail and shelter use, and also correc*ng what probably
should have been corrected before - the Club launched a variety of programs; the
removal of dumps at shelters and promo*on of the "carry-in, carry-out' policy; the
sta*oning of caretakers at heavily used shelters to maintain the facili*es.
[GB 21st Edi*on 1977]

<!-- page: 156 -->
Presently there are over seventy GMC overnight sites, spaced no more than a
moderate day's hike apart, along the Long Trail System. These range from *ny,
enclosed lodges to three-sided lean-tos and tent sites. All sites, although
primi*ve, have a water source (purity and reliability cannot be guaranteed) and
privy. Visitors must carry food, a backpacking stove and overnight gear.

The cabins we built had three categories. A shelter was an open front Adirondack
style structure. A camp was a four-sided structure, about 12 by 14 feet with upper
and lower bunks in each back corner. Each bunk could accommodate two
comfortably or three very tolerant and close friends. I never heard the six hikers or
six porcupines descrip*on (a story Roy Buchanan told). Each boTom bunk was
extended at the foot and the free side to provided sea*ng. The sea*ng on the free
side served as the sea*ng for the table which was between the two sets of bunks.
This bunk and table arrangement freed the front part of the cabin for general use.
A stove was placed at one end of the general use area. A camp had two or three
glassed windows, one in back of the stove. one at the/ end of the table and the
third one, if used, at the other end of the general use area. The other camp
openings were closable by hinged, solid shuTers. A lodge was similar to a camp,
but somewhat larger to accommodate more people. Some of the lodges in those
early days had full-sized kitchen ranges brought in in pieces on the backs of some
of the more dedicated hikers. [Reminiscences about Roy Buchanan and The LT By
Chet Buchanan. 1998]

The overnight structures along the trail are designated as shelters. camps and
lodges. Shelters usually have open fronts in the Adirondack style. Some camps are
enclosed and have doors and glazed windows. The larger enclosed structures are
lodges. Any excep*ons to these classiﬁca*ons are noted. In the trail descrip*on
the word "shelter" is also used in referring to trail structures in general.

These facili*es are ﬁrst-come, ﬁrst-served. Please ﬁt fellow hikers in. Use of any
overnight facility is limited to three consecu*ve nights.

Groups should follow group hiking guidelines and restric*ons noted in the Long
Trail Guidebook. A moderate fee is charged at overnight sites with caretakers.

To improve outhouse eﬃciency and reduce odors, whenever possible urinate in
the woods well away from water sources. As part of its waste management

<!-- page: 157 -->
eﬀorts, the GMC composts sewage at some overnight sites. Please follow any
special instruc*ons posted on the outhouse door.

To help support this important program, a modest overnight use fee is charged at
caretaker sites:

$4 for non-members; GMC'ers with membership cards pay a $3 fee except at
shelters on Na*onal Forest land where the discount is not allowed. (These fees
were for the 1996 season and are subject to change.) This is a use fee and applies
to tent as well as shelter users.

If you packed it in, pack it out! Help by picking up trash others have leG behind.

Use a backpacking stove. Wood ﬁres are strongly discouraged and are prohibited
at high eleva*on. Fires severely impact surrounding areas by deple*ng vegeta*on,
causing soil compac*on and erosion. Fires should be built only in ﬁreplaces
provided. Keep ﬁres small. Bum only dead and downed wood. Never cut live
branches or trees for ﬁrewood.

Travel and camp in small groups. Four to six hikers to a party is ideal; ten is
maximum for overnight and twenty-ﬁve for day use. Be considerate of other
hikers and make space for them in shelters.

Camp at established overnight sites. Use the exis*ng shelters and ten*ng areas
whenever possible to avoid widespread impact.

Wash and rinse dishes away from open water. Use the designated wash pit or, if
primi*ve camping, wash dishes and "self" at least two hundred feet (seventy-ﬁve
paces) from any water source. Avoid soap as it is diﬃcult to rinse and is more
oGen the source of gastrointes*nal distress than water contamina*on. If you use
soap, use it sparingly; a few drops go a long way.

Use the privy if the site has one. Otherwise, dispose of human and pet waste in a
"cat hole" between four and six inches deep and at least two hundred feet
(seventy-ﬁve paces) away from water sources. Bury toilet paper. Treat your dog's
waste in the same way as your own - bury it. And, above *mberline, carry it out.

<!-- page: 158 -->
Carry out old tampons and sanitary napkins. Carry a personal trash bag for this
purpose. Please do not dispose of these products in outhouses.

Don't carve your name or anything else into trees or shelters. Although it may be
fun to leave your mark, please refrain. It alters the experience of fellow hikers and
is an insult to the careful work that went into these crea*ons. Respect other hikers
and wildlife. Travel and camp quietly.

Pets are best leG at home. If you bring a dog along, make sure it is well trained
and keep it away from water sources. Leash it above tree line and at shelters
when you cannot give your dog your full aTen*on. Be thoughjul of shelter mates.
Pets are likely to encounter their own trail problems, especially painful baTles
with porcupines.

Leave no trace. Leave no signs of your presence anywhere along the trail.

WATER - Although the GMC makes every eﬀort to locate shelters and campsites
near water sources, the quality and quan*ty of water cannot be guaranteed.
Water may be polluted, and, during dry weather, water sources may fail. Areas
that are par*cularly prone to water shortages are noted in the guidebooks. [LT GB
24 24th Edi*on 1996]

FIRE STOVE WANTED The spookiest tale of anonymous Long Trail philanthropy on
record comes from the southern stretches when a cabin was furnished with a
broken-down stove that wouldn't bum, wouldn't cook. wouldn't heat Hikers
seeded the expected diatribes against it in the register.

Finally, in despera*on and a liTle anger, one sec*on oﬃcer ripped a sheet from
the book, scribbled across it: "If you don't like this stove, please buy us a new
one." He placed the invita*on on the cold stove and broke camp. Within a week
two heroes came along, sized up the situa*on, walked back to town and brought
back a brand-new stove. May their tribe increase. [LT News, Feb. 1941]

Adopters

<!-- page: 160 -->
The Club's Adopt-A-Shelter Program now has basic policy guidelines for its
opera*on but needs somebody to assume responsibility of its administra*on.
Under the program, GMC'ers, individually, family, or small group may adopt a
shelter by performing certain inspec*on and maintenance services on a regular
basis for a single shelter or lodge.

At its mee*ng Jan. 22, 1977, the Club's Board of Trustees adopted the following
administra*ve guidelines for opera*on of the program:

   1. Approval must be received from the Sec*on responsible for the par*cular
      shelter before that shelter can be included in the program.
   2. Adopters will be expected to inspect the shelter on a regular basis, and
      promptly submit a standardized report of the inspec*on to the responsible
      Sec*on with a copy to the Program Director.
   3. Work by adopters will be limited to that involving no expenditure of money
      except by prior arrangement with the responsible Sec*on.
   4. The assignment of a shelter adopter does not supplant the Sec*on's
      maintenance obliga*ons and responsibili*es for that shelter.

At that same mee*ng, President Ralph Bryant announced that Trustee Donald M.
Wallace had resigned as director of the program. [LT News, May 1977]

Outhouses
Outhouses are an integral part of all shelters. Unlike dumps, they are a human
necessity and most unglamorous to take care for all caretakers of shelters. Much
has been wriTen about them in shelter journals and ou*ng logs. Many scien*ﬁc
minds have aTempted to devise more caretaker friendly outhouses; however, I
think that an outhouse is an outhouse, is an outhouse. They s*nk, they aTract
criTers, toilet paper gets wet, it is unsightly, they ﬁll up, they have to be moved,
the doors don't open, they make a lot of noise at night, they are a nightmare for
trail & shelter workers, and they create a tremendous amount of work. SHIT
HAPPENS!

To give one an idea of the importance of outhouse along the trails and at shelters
here are excerpts from the Sterling Sec*on of the GMC Ou*ng Log 1971 to 1977:
Work Trip June 12, 1976-(2) Fred Judd repaired several holes in the back wall of
the Whiteface outhouse made by porcupines - all except one hole which was
purposefully leG. Fred's theory is that a key aTrac*on to the porkies is (ugh!) the
human dung in the outhouse pit; they thus gnaw through the wall to get into the
pit. Therefore, if you leave a hole for them for that purpose, they won't gnaw

<!-- page: 164 -->
elsewhere. This, Fred did with a handsomely designed, well carpentered hole at
the ground line. Further inspec*ons will be made to determine the outcome of
the experiment... Work Trip July 9, 1977- eliminated was the porcupine access
hole provided on a trial basis on June 12, 1976. It seems clear to Hagerman that
the porkies were more interested in gnawing on the outhouse boards than the
human dung and thus the access hole was helping rather than diver*ng the
porcupines in their con*nuing onslaughts and should be eliminated. [Bob
Hagerman 7/9/77]

Caretaker Program

<!-- page: 166 -->
The Caretaker Program is not new. From the early days GMC caretakers were
intermiTently on duty charging fees and maintaining the buildings at a number of
lodges on the Tran. Today, with some twenty caretakers. The program represents a
coordinated eﬀort by the GMC not only to maintain the shelters and trails, but to
educate the many inexperienced hikers through informal conversa*on and
example.

The Caretaker is responsible for the upkeep of his or her shelter. but does not
provide services that are normally the hiker's responsibility.

GMC caretakers are experienced hikers. They are happy to provide backpacking
sugges*ons and *ps, as well as basic informa*on on the Club and the Trail. They
are dedicated to the premise that most hikers, when provided with the proper
informa*on, will seek to minimize their impact on the rail environment.

There will be a slight increase in caretaker salaries from $175. to $200. on average.
A con*nuing decrease in the number of caretaker applicants seem to indicate that
higher salaries are necessary to aTract and retain qualiﬁed people. [LT News, May
1977]

   Green Mountain Caretaker
   Howard Van Benthuysen
   [Ridge Lines; Vol 2; No2; Oct 1972]


   During the last summer l had the express pleasure of working for
   Kenn Boyd and the GMC as a caretaker at Gorham Lodge on
   camel's Hump. I moved into my mountain retreat during the
   Memorial Day weekend and reluctantly departed on August 25th.
   My du;es usually began around 7 a.m. though some;mes I arose
   before 5. I kept my guests supplied with ﬁrewood, water for
   cooking, drinking and dishwashing and shared my two-burner
   Coleman stove. Interes;ngly enough, there were 121 portable
   stoves brought into Gorham. These ranged in sophis;ca;on from
   Sterno, through Svea 123's (the most popular but not the best), to
   the heavy and bulky Coleman single burner stoves and alcohol
   burners. Thus, the wood stove was only used ﬁve ;mes.


   This trend toward portable stove usage on the Long Trail is
   especially signiﬁcant to the Mt. Mansﬁeld and Camel's Hump
   areas. Both of these areas are State parks. But, more important
   within was of the tracts are small areas which have been
   designated as "natural areas." In each case "natural areas means
   those regions on the summits which are covered with rare and
   fragile alpine vegeta;on. This designa;on, promulgated by the
   Legislature in coopera;on with the Department of Forests and
   Parks, is designed to preserve and protect the few unspoiled lands
   le> in the State. Thus. in both of these areas, and in fact on all
   State land. it is unlawful to build open ﬁres. pick or destroy plant
   life or camp in other than authorized sites. You are (Probably
   familiar with the reasons for these regula;ons: overuse, misuse by

<!-- page: 169 -->
the proverbial "few." ﬁre hazard and the growing desire to retain
       some truly natural areas.


       In addi;on to my rou;ne du;es of lodge maintenance and clean-
       up, I was able to ﬁnd a handful of sunny days in which to re-blaze
       several of the side trails in the area, do some corduroying and
       water barring and remove the dump from Gorham. Another of my
       responsibili;es was the weekly inspec;on and repair of Wiley
       Lodge, a small frame lodge 4.3 miles northwest of the Hump.


       Over the course of my 90-day period of caretaking, I greeted a total
       of 620 overnight guests. This is an increase of roughly 100 over last
       year's recorded use for Gorham Lodge. In addi;on to 620 overnight
       guests, I had 304 hikers pass through during July and August who
       did not stay overnight. Of the 620, 44% were from Vermont and
       16% from MassachuseMs. Next came Connec;cut, New York, New
       Jersey, and Canada Guests signed in from such exo;c places as
       Hawaii, Iceland, New Zealand, and Italy. Nineteen other states
       were represented.


       I took the job as caretaker because I felt that in this day and age, I
       could not aﬀord to pass up on opportunity to live by myself in
       unspoiled Vermont woods. I just did not think. that I would have
       many more opportuni;es to do this type of thing. I genuinely
       enjoyed my summer on the Long Trail; living in the midst of such
       beauty, dealing with pleasant and interes;ng people and having
       countless hours for reﬂec;on and study - all combined to make my
       care-taking experience highly rewarding and unforgeMable.

       ReﬂecCons of a GMC Caretaker
       Peg Whitson [Ridge Lines; Vol 4, No 4; March 1975]
       Bolton and Me
       It was gone - much too quickly. It was not a spectacular summer
       like ra>ing down the Grand Canyon. It was a summer of peace and
       people and sharing. A summer; of feeling no need for spending or

<!-- page: 171 -->
TV or loud par;es. I missed ice-cold drinks and a good reading
       light. Nothing more.
       I met over 800 people, only 9 of whom I wouldn't welcome back.
       Are people diﬀerent on the trail or does a diﬀerent sort of person
       use the trail?
       It was a summer of hard physical labor - coupled with complete
       relaxa;on beside a campﬁre or a secluded swimming hole. I have a
       renewed faith in the human race, especially young people. My
       sense of awe was unceasing at the natural wonders of a newly
       discovered waterfall, a golden halo over Camel's Hump, or a moss-
       covered rock deep in the woods.
       I feel I have served my guests well. More apprecia;on I have never
       received. I've learned to be generous with what I have. I've given
       food, stove, candles, and ;me. I have received in return, more food,
       money, fuel, and best of all. much good will.

       Trail Bellhop
       by Dane Shortsleeve printed in LT News, May 1941.
       The caretaker's job - or rather his season - begins about the ﬁrst of
       July when he ﬁnds himself alone for perhaps the ﬁrst ;me in his life
       - miles from the nearest house and telephone and des;ned to be
       completely on his own for two months. The ﬁrst two or three days
       are spent in good old-fashioned house cleaning from ﬂoor to
       ra>ers: under the bunks as well as the bunks themselves,
       cupboards, stove, and even last corner, where a mouse may have
       wintered, or a porky indulged in a chaw. The maMresses are aired
       in the sun, and wood rustled up; that means good wood, for on
       arrival one expects to ﬁnd only a meager supply of wet birch. A>er
       gathering and bucking a goodly quan;ty of rock maple, the
       caretaker is faced with the problem of outside cleaning around the
       lodge: cuzng grass, cleaning the spring, placing Trail signs, and
       clearing trail of dead wood felled by the winter.

<!-- page: 181 -->
A>er the ﬁrst few days, the caretaker just has to seMle down to a
summer's rou;ne. Besides the daily wood-gathering and cleaning,
a good supply of water has to be kept in stock, blankets aired, and
the lodge in good shape for hikers of all types. Aside from his du;es
at the Trail lodge there is ;me for hikes over the mountain trails.
And plenty of rainy days may be counted on for reading books and
cooking appe;zers for a change. Frequent hikes to the village for
mail and camp supplies break the monotony of a long spell of
inclement weather when Lodge visitors are few.
An occasional visit from a neighboring caretaker is a welcome
occasion for swapping notes and comparing observa;ons. Long
a>er you have passed on over another peak and to another lodge,
the impressions you le> behind are quietly recalled, your stories
passed on to be swapped for new ones, and the candy bar you le>
on the bunk apprecia;vely munched.
One would expect to ﬁnd Long Trailers more alike, but they are
really all diﬀerent. The principal things they have in common are a
love of the great out-of-doors, a yen for unspoiled nature, a
friendly feeling for their fellow Trail lovers, and usually an appe;te
to be proud of. In types they range from Cub Scouts to members of
Mountain Climbing Clubs from far oﬀ sec;ons of the country.
In helping to ﬁnd storage space for packs and equipment, no one
has a beMer opportunity to take note on carry-alls than the
caretaker. The varie;es of packs are innumerable: compact
trapper's ounits, complicated canvas jobs, blanket rolls, and all
styles of pack baskets. For sleeping some depend on ren;ng
blankets at lodges where nighnall overtakes them, while others
carry their many-fold lightweight sleeping bags, and occasionally a
neophyte appears with a sleeping ounit the size of a beauty-rest
maMress.
There is ample ;me, too, during the summer, for close study of bird
life: frequent glimpses of partridge or a pheasant among the thick
trees and brush of the unspoiled mountain sec;ons, and iden;fying
the calls of birds, the blue jay and the sweet song of the Hermit
thrush mingling with the music of a mountain rill. And the

<!-- page: 182 -->
caretaker's job also oﬀers a rare opportunity for prac;ce of Boy
Scout lore.
One can recall quiet nights lying in his bunk when the only sounds
breaking the silence of the mountains were the screech owls calling
back and forth across the valley and other nights when the roar of
wind and rain in the forest made the lodge seem like a parlor in the
wilderness. Climbers come and go, but the real thrills of the Green
Mountains belong to the caretakers who spend a full season living
among the woods, alone or with company.


Problems for the Trustees
An old diﬃculty that most of the Sec;ons have where the trail is in
good deer-hun;ng territory is the use or rather abuse of the closed
camps by deer-hunters. For some reason which we are unable to
fathom some of these hun;ng par;es seem unable to ﬁnd fuel and
use the bunks and tables instead. The equipment in the Sunrise
Camp has had to be replaced four diﬀerent years, and others have
had similar diﬃculty. Perhaps the solu;on is the one adopted by
the Manchester & Proctor Sec;ons with the Bromley Lodge and the
Noyes Pond Camp. These have been rented for several years at a
modest fee to par;es of reliable hunters, and the results have been
excellent. Another problem is that of the par;es who go in to one
of the camps during the summer and stay there for weeks at a
;me. A recent leMer justly complains: "The only unpleasantness of
our summer was encountered during the hike on the Trail. At two
points we found shelters occupied by people who were not hikers.
At Pico Shelter we found a group of seven boys from Rutland who
were spending ﬁve days at the place, and who showed liMle
considera;on to others who came to the shelter. At LiMle Rock Pond
a couple had set up light housekeeping in the shelter in early June,
and we found them there late in August. These people were from
Brooklyn and had spent the en;re summer camping at the shelter."
This use of our camps seems to be a growing one, and one that is
very diﬃcult to control unless we can have many caretakers.
